[[P1031]]SECTION VI 

CHAPTER XXVI 

[[Tūntrik|Tāntrik]] doctrines and [[Dharmasastra|Dharmaśāstra]] 

In H. of Dh. vol. II. p. 739 while dealing with the worship of Durgā, who is also worshipped as [[Sakti|Śakti]] (cosmic power or energy ), it was stated that the influence of [[Sākta|Śākta]] worship has [[boon|been]] great throughout India and a promise was given that a brief treatment of [[Sāktiem|Śāktism]] would follow in a later volume. It is now time to deal with Śāktas and Tantras, which exercised some influence over the Purāṇas and directly and through the Purāṇas over Indian religious ritual and practices in the medieval ages. 

There is a vast literature on Tantras, published and unpublished. There are Buddhist Tantras, Hindu [[Trantras|Tantras]] and Jaina Tantras. There is a philosophical or spiritual side in some of the Tantras which has not been much studied except by Arthur Avalon, B. Bhattacharya and a few others. In the popular mind Tantras [[bave|have]] been associated with the worship of [[Sakti|Śakti]] ( Goddess [[Kāli|Kālī]] ), with mudrās, mantras, [[mandalas|maṇḍalas]], the five makāras, the Dakṣiṇamārga and the Vāma-mārga, and magic practices for acquiring supernatural powers. All that can and will be attempted here is to trace briefly the origin of [[Sāktism|Śāktism]] and Tantra and point out some of the ways in which tantra has entered into Hindu ritual directly and [[indireotly|indirectly]] through the Purāṇas. 

The Amarakośa states that tantra means ' principal matter or part', 'siddhānta' ([[i. e,|i. e.]] doctrine or śāstra ), a loom or [[paren phernalia|paraphernalia]]. But it does not state that tantra means a certain peculiar class of works. Therefore, the inference is not altogether unjustifiable that in its time works bearing the peculiar [[charaoter|character]] of what are called Tantras were either not composed or had not at least attained great vogue. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 71. 9 the word '[[ |]]tantra' [[ooours|occurs]] and appears to mean a loom. ' These ignorant[^1660] men do not move about lower down ( in this world ) nor in a higher

[[P1032]]world, being neither (learned) brāhmaṇas nor [[some-extracting|soma-extracting]] priests; these resort to speech (of a vile kind) and with that vile speech they engage in handling ploughs and [[looms!|looms'.]] The [[Atharveveda|Atharvaveda]] (X. 7. 42) ('tantram-eke [[yurati|yuvati]] virūpe [[abhya krāmam|abhyā-krāmam]] vayataḥ [[san-mayūkham'|ṣaṇ-mayūkham']] ) employs the word tantra in the same sense and so does the Tai. Br. II. 5. 5. 3 in a closely similar passage. Pāṇini ([[V.| V.]] 2. 70 ) derives the word 'tantraka' ([[ |]]a cloth that is recently taken away from a loom ) from 'tantra'. The [[Ap. Sr.|Āp. Śr.]] employs the word[^1661] tantra in the sense of 'procedure containing many parts'. The [[sān. Sr.|Śāṅ. Śr.]] applies the word tantra to what being once done serves the purpose of many other actions. The Mahābhāṣya on Pāṇini IV. 2. 60 and Vārtika [['sarvasāder dvigośca lah'cites 'garvatantrah'|'sarvasāder-dvigośca laḥ' cites 'sarvatantraḥ']] and 'dvitantraḥ' as examples meaning 'one who has studied all the tantras' or 'one who has studied two tantras', tantra probably meaning '[[siddbānta|siddhānta]]'. In Yāj. I. 228 ([['tantram va vaisva.devikam'|'tantraṁ vā vaiśva-devikam']]) the word tantra is employed in the sense in which the commentary on the [[Sān. Sr.|Śāṅ. Śr.]] takes it. The 15th [[adhikarapa|adhikaraṇa]] of [[Kautilya's|Kauṭilya's]] Arthaśāstra bears the title 'Tantrayukti' (vide J. O. R., Madras, vol. 4, 1930, p. 82 ff) meaning the main canons or propositions or principles of exposition [[cf|of]] a śāstra. Caraka (Siddhisthāna, chap. 12. 40-45) also speaks of '36 tantrasya yuktayaḥ' and [[Susruta|Suśruta]] ([[ |]]Uttara-tantra, chap. 65 ) names 32 tantrayuktis. In [[Bphaspati|Bṛhaspati]] and [[Katyāyana|Kātyāyana]] and in the [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] the word tantra is employed in the sense of 'doctrine' or 'śāstra'. [[Sabara|Śabara]] in his _bhāṣya_ on Jaimini XI. [[I.|1.]] 1 remarks that when [[and|any]] thing or act, once done, becomes useful in several[^1662] matters, that is called 'tantra'. [[Saṅkarācārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] in several passages of his _bhāṣya_ on the [[Vedanta.'|Vedānta-]]sūtras speaks of the Sāṅkhya system as [[sānkhya-tantra|sāṅkhya-tantra]] and of the [[Purvammāmgā|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]] as [['prathama-tantra '|'prathama-tantra'.]][^1663] In the Kālikā-

[[P1033]]purāṇa (87. 130) the works of [[Usanas and Bphaspati on Rajaniti ( the science of statecraft)|Uśanas and Bṛhaspati on Rājanīti ( the science of statecraft )]] are called tantras and in [[92.2|92. 2]] the Viṣṇudharmottara-purāṇa is called tantra. In all these cases the peculiar meaning attached to the word tantra in medieval times does not occur. 

It is difficult to [[determina|determine]] the exact[^1664] time when the word tantra came to [[bo|be]] employed in the sense in which it is used in the [[80-called|so-called]] tantra literature nor is it possible to decide what people first introduced tantra principles and practices or where they first arose. M. M. Haraprasad Shastri was inclined to hold that the principles and practices of tantra came to India from [[out side|outside]] and he relies mainly on a verse in the Kubjikāmatatantra[^1665]

LXXIX, for the verse in Kubjikāmata, the ms. of which is written in late Gapta characters (i, em about 7th century A. D.). Dr. B. Bhattacharya appears to hold the same opinion (p: 43 of the work cited in the preceding note). Arthur Avalon in Mahānirvāṇatantra (3rd ed, of 1953 p. 560) holds that tantra was brought into India from Chaldea or Sakadvipa. In 'Modern Review' for 1934 pp. 150–156 Prof. N, N. Choudhari tries to prove that Indian tāntricism bas its origin in the Bon religion of Tibet. He relies on the Tibetan tradition of Asanga baving introduced Tantricism in India. But that tradition is mainly based on Taranath's History of Buddhism. Lama Taranath was born in 1573 A, D. (1575 A, D, according to some) and completed bis history in 1608 i. e. he wrote about twelve hundred years after Asanga. Prof. Choudhuri further relies on the colophon to Ekajatāsādbana (in Sadhana māla No. 127, 314 Taulantay safna). But this sentence is dropped 

in three out of the eight mss. on which the edition is based. Prof. Choudburi further asserts that the guru's position 'in tantra' is neither Vaidika nor Paurāṛika. Here he is wrong. The Vidyāsūkta in Nirakta (II. 4) and the passage of the Svetāśvatara Upanisad quoted in bote 1540 make the Vaidika position of the guru clear. As for the Paorānika position of guru, vide the Lingaparāṇa (quoted in note 1718 below) and Devi bhāgavata (XI. 1. 49.Gurur-brahma' &c. in the same pota) and the Vorse 'यस्य देवे पर भक्तिर्यथा देखे तथा शुरौ तस्यैते कथिता पर्याः प्रकाशन्ते महात्मन: चेताया 34. VI, 23 and S . 392, 6.|I. H. Q. Vol. IX p. 358 f. n. 'गच्छ त्वं भारते वर्षे अधिकाराय सर्वतः । पीठोपपीठक्षेत्रेषु कुरु सृष्टिमनेकधा ॥'. Vide H. P. Sastri's Cat. of Palm-leaf mss. in Nepal Durbar Library (Calcutta, 1905), Preface p. LXXIX, for the verse in Kubjikāmata, the ms. of which is written in late Gupta characters (i. e. about 7th century A. D.). Dr. B. Bhattacharya appears to hold the same opinion (p. 43 of the work cited in the preceding note). Arthur Avalon in Mahānirvāṇatantra (3rd ed. of 1953 p. 560) holds that tantra was brought into India from Chaldea or Śākadvīpa. In 'Modern Review' for 1934 pp. 150–156 Prof. N. N. Choudhuri tries to prove that Indian tāntricism has its origin in the Bon religion of Tibet. He relies on the Tibetan tradition of Asaṅga having introduced Tāntricism in India. But that tradition is mainly based on Taranath's History of Buddhism. Lama Taranath was born in 1573 A. D. (1575 A. D. according to some) and completed his history in 1608 i. e. he wrote about twelve hundred years after Asaṅga. Prof. Choudhuri further relies on the colophon to Ekajaṭāsādhana (in Sādhanamālā No. 127, आर्यनागार्जुनपादैर्भोटेषु उद्धृतमिति). But this sentence is dropped in three out of the eight mss. on which the edition is based. Prof. Choudhuri further asserts that the guru's position 'in tantra' is neither Vaidika nor Paurāṇika. Here he is wrong. The Vidyāsūkta in Nirukta (II. 4) and the passage of the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad quoted in note 1540 make the Vaidika position of the guru clear. As for the Paurāṇika position of guru, vide the Liṅgapurāṇa (quoted in note 1718 below) and Devībhāgavata (XI. 1. 49 'Gurur-brahmā' &c. in the same note) and the verse 'यस्य देवे परा भक्तिर्यथा देवे तथा गुरौ । तस्यैते कथिता ह्यर्थाः प्रकाशन्ते महात्मनः ॥' श्वेताश्व० उप. VI. 23 and अग्निपु. 392. 6.]] 

[[P1034]]which says 'go thou to the country of [[Bharata|Bhārata]] for [[exorotsing|exercising]] dominance on all sides and bring about new creation in [[varioua|various]] ways in [[pithas, upapithas|pīṭhas, upapīṭhas]] and kṣetras'. With all respect to that great scholar it has to be said that the passage does not affirm that tantra principles were then [[unknowe|unknown]] in India and were first introduced in [[Bbārata|Bhārata]] after that verse was uttered. That passage could very well have been uttered even when tantra practices had already taken hold of people's minds in India and the reference to [[pithas aad ksetras|pīṭhas and kṣetras]] ( in the verse ) clearly indicates that what is meant is only a record of an existing fact, just as the Purāṇas speak in a prophetic vein about what is past. It is possible that a few mystic practices of [[Rulācāra|Kulācāra]] or Vāmācāra owe their origin to foreign elements or sources. But the one [[Verse|verse]] on which M. M. H. P. Shastri relied is far too inadequate for [[proung|proving]] this. The Rudrayāmala ( ed. by Jivananda, 1892) contains a panegyric of the [[Atharvayeda|Atharvaveda]] ( 17th [[patala,|paṭala,]] verse 4), saying that all gods, all beings (on land, in water and air ), [[ali|all]] sages, [[Kamavidyā and Mahavidyā|Kāmavidyā and Mahāvidyā]] dwell in it; verses 10-17 dwell upon the mysterious [[Kundalini,|Kuṇḍalinī,]] verses 31 ff dilate upon Yogic practices and six cakras in the body, verses 51-53 mention Kāmarūpa, [[Jalandhara, Purnagiri, Uddiyāna,|Jālandhara, Pūrṇagiri, Uḍḍiyāna,]] and a few [[otherg 89 Kalika pithas,|others as Kālikā pīṭhas,]] Bagchi in 'Studies in Tantra' pp. 45-55 ) adduces some evidence of foreign elements in the tāntrik doctrines. The Rudrayāmala[^1666] (17th [[Patala,|Paṭala,]] [[verges|verses]] 119-125 ) states that [[Mabāvidyā|Mahāvidyā]] appeared to sage [[Vasiṣtha|Vasiṣṭha]] and asked him to go to Cinadeśa and Buddha, who is said to have taught [[Vasistha|Vasiṣṭha]] the [[K'aula|Kaula]] path, Yoga practices for the attainment of siddhis ( extraordinary powers) and directed him to make use of five makāras ( madya &c.) in his [[sūdhanā|sādhanā]] for becoming a perfect Yogin. All this shows that [[pithas|pīṭhas]] flourished in India when the [[Rudrayāmāla|Rudrayāmala]] was composed, that Tāntrik practices had grown in China or Tibet and that Buddha was deemed to have taught those practices, which is a libel and a vile travesty of Buddha's noble teaching.

[[P1035]]Magic spells are found in plenty in the [[Atharvayeda|Atharvaveda]] and some mystic words or syllables are used even in the Ṛgveda; e. g. the word [['yaṣat,|'vaṣaṭ']] occurs in Ṛg. VII. 99. 7, VII. 100. 7 and other verses and the word svāhā[^1667] occurs over a dozen times in the Ṛgveda (e. g. in I. 13. 12, V. 5. 11, VII. 2. 11). A sleep-inducing spell occurs in Ṛg. VII. 55. 5–8,[^1668] which verses occur also in the Atharvaveda IV. 5. 6, 5, 1, 3 and which spell [[waa|was]] probably employed by a purohita to put to sleep some noble man complaining of [[sleepless11888|sleeplessness]] at night. Some Western scholars have held that this hymn is a lover's charm for [[&|a]] clandestine meeting with his lady-love. But the whole hymn [[ban|has]] hardly any words of love in it and the author is not able to accept that theory as proved. [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] X. 145 is a hymn to be employed against a co-wife, the first verse of which is 'I dig up this herb, which is a most powerful [[crespor,|creeper,]] by which (a woman) injures her co-wife and by which she secures her husband ( to herself alone )'. 

The Ṛgveda frequently mentions magicians who appear to have been mostly non-Aryans described as adeva (godless), anṛtadeva ( worshipping false gods ), śiśnadeva (lecherous, [[Rs.|Ṛg.]] VII. 21. 5, X. 99. 3). For reasons of space, it is not possible to go into great details. Tāntrik works describe the six [[cruel.|cruel]] acts which will be dealt with later on. In the Vedic times it was supposed that some wicked people could by charms and [[incantan tions|incantations]] bring about the death of men and animals or make [[thema|them]] ill. Two long hymns ([[VI.|VII.]] 104 and X. 87 both containing 25 [[Verses)|verses)]] are enough to show how the [[Rgyeda|Ṛgveda]] people were afraid of black magic. Both hymns are full of the words ' Yātudhāna' (one who employs black magic) and 'rakṣas' (devil or evil spirit), the word yātu being the same as [['"jadu'|'jādu']] (employed in Marathi and other regional Indian languages). There were female evil spirits also [[oalled|called]] [['pisaoi'|'piśācī']] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 133. 5 'O Indra! destroy the reddish very powerful [[pisāoi|piśācī]] and kill all evil spirits )'. A few verses from the Ṛgveda may be [[tradslated|translated]]

Vide Tantrik Texts, vol. 7, -whatever to called caret, et aad is, also interface VI. 62-63,|In the Tantras the word 'svāhā' in mantras is indicated by such a word as 'wife of Agni'. या! "मोठेशया पोशया नाहीस्तल्याशीवरी। सियो या पुण्यगन्धास्ता सर्वाः स्थापयामसि । R. VII. 35. 5-8.|सस्तु माता सस्तु पिता सस्तु श्वा सस्तु विश्पतिः । ससन्तु सर्वे ज्ञातयः सस्त्वयमभितो जनः ॥ य आस्ते यश्च चरति यश्च पश्यति नो जनः । तेषां सं हन्मो अक्षाणि यथेदं हर्म्यं तथा । ... प्रोष्ठेशया वह्येशया नारीस्तल्पशीवरीः । स्त्रियो याः पुण्यगन्धास्ताः सर्वाः स्वापयामसि ॥ ऋ. VII. 55. 5–8.]] 

[[P1036]]here. 'May I (Vasiṣṭha) die this very day if I [[bo &|be a]] practitioner of black magic or if I have scorched the life of any person; may he, who falsely called me a practitioner of black [[magio,|magic,]] lose [[bis|his]] ten sons; may Indra kill with a terrible weapon him who called me [[yūtudhāna,|yātudhāna,]] though I am not so and who, being himself a rakṣas, declares himself to be [[purs;|pure;]] may he, being most wretched, fall below all beings (Rg. VII. 104. 15–16); O Maruts! may you spread in different places among the people, and wish to seize the devils and pound to dust the devils (rakṣasaḥ) who [[Assuming|assuming]] the form of birds fly about at night and who, when the sacrifice is shining, produce deadly obstacles ( ibid. verse 18 ); O Indra! kill the male practitioner of black magic and also the female ( magician) that destroys with wiles; may the ( devils ) worshippers of foolish deities perish with their necks cut off; and may they not be able to see the sun rising (Rg. VII. 104. 24); O Agni, split the skin of the yātudhāna, may thy [[destruc. tiye|destructive]] bolt kill him by its heat; O [[Jātapedas /|Jātavedas!]] shatter his joints, may some carnivorous beast longing for flesh seek ( devour ) the broken ( yātudhāna ); O Agni! shatter the yātudhānas by your heat, and the rakṣas by your glow and destroy the worshippers of foolish gods ([[mūradevūn)|mūradevān)]] and, shining towards those that feed on the lives of men ([[asulgpah),|asutṛpaḥ),]] shatter them' (Rg. X. 87. 5 and 14). 

• In the Āp. Gṛ. (III. 9. 5–8) it is said that the plant used by the co-wife is called [[Pathā|Pāṭhā]] and the hymn (Rg. X. 145 ) is employed for securing domination over the husband and for harming a co-wife. Rg. I. 191 is a charm against various poisons. In the Atharvaveda there are numerous [[hymds|hymns]] styled 'śatrunāśana' (destructive of enemies) e. g. II. 12–24, III. 6, IV. 3 and 40, V. 8, VI. 6, 65–67 and 134. Atharva II. 11 is styled 'Kṛtyā-dūṣaṇa' (counteracting black magic). A [[fow|few]] of the typical verses may be cited[^1669] here. 'Employ [[magio|magic]] spells against him, who hates us and whom we hate; attain (i. e. dominate) him who is superior and surpass him who is (our) equal'; 'O Soma! strike in the mouth with your thunder-bolt him who speaks evil of us that speak what is good and may he, being crushed, run away'. The [[Sukranitisārs|Śukranītisāra]] (ed. by G. Oppert, 1882 ) provides that the Tantras are the Upaveda of the

[[P1037]]Atharvaveda. Atharva III. 25 and VI. 130 are spells respectively employed by a man and a woman to soften the heart of the person loved; Atharva II. 30 and 31 are charms for driving away or destroying worms that cause diseases and V. 36 is a charm against [[pisācas|piśācas]] (goblins ).[^1670] The sound 'phaṭ' occurs in Vāj. S. In the [[Ap. Sr. Sātra|Āp. Śr. Sūtra]] phaṭ is employed in offering Soma stalks in [[abhi cūra|abhicāra]] ( employment of spells for a malevolent purpose ). Phaṭ is a sound frequently employed in the worship of Devī in Tantra works. But no direct connection or line of evolution from the Atharvaveda to the Tantras can be traced. The [[Tattvasangraha|Tattvasaṅgraha]] of [[śāntarakṣita|Śāntarakṣita]] (705–762 A. D.) connects even Buddha with [[magio|magic]] practices. It says 'all wise [[mon|men]] declare that it is dharma from which results worldly prosperity and the highest beatitude. Seen results such as intelligence, health, rulership are produced by properly observing the rules about mantras, yoga and the like declared by him (i. e.[^1671] by Buddha)'. But one cannot place implicit reliance on any writer's statement made more than a thousand [[yean|years]] after the event or person referred to in it. There are, however, stories in the Pali sacred books about the cultivation of magic powers among Buddha's own disciples, [[o. g.|e. g.]] the story of [[Bharadvāja 1672|Bhāradvāja]][^1672] who rose in the air for [[&|a]] bowl carved out of very fragrant

The word spycy has been variously interpreted by the commentators of Kanada's sātra, bat gene rally Speat means 'wordly happiness or prosperity '; compare ma's नाट्यशान 'विवाहमसवावाहसमोवाम्युदयादिषु। विनोदकरणं चैव वृत्तमेतस्मकीर्तितम् ।' chap. IV, 263 (G. O. S.). The first verse appears to have been based on the वैशेषिकसूत्र I. 1. 1–2 'अथातो धर्म व्याख्यास्यामः । यतोऽभ्युदयनिःश्रेयससिद्धिः स धर्मः ।'. [^1672]: [[The story of Pandola Bhāradvāja, a disciple of the Blessed One (Buddha ) rising in the air, takiog the bowl and going thrice round the city of Rājagrha in the air, is parrated in Callavagga (S. B. E. Vol. XX pp 78 ff). But it is said there that Buddha rebuked his disciple, ordered that bowl to be broken and reduced to powder.]] 

[[P1038]]sandalwood. Further, there is a story of miraculous powers possessed by all the members of the family of a layman called [[Mendaka|Meṇḍaka]] (viz. himself, his wife, son and daughter-in-law ) in Mahāvagga VI. 34. 1 ff. ([[8.|S.]] B. E. vol. [[XVI.|XVII.]] pp. 121 ff). [[Honey|Here]] again we have to remember that there is nothing in the [[Tipitaka|Tipiṭaka]] or any early Buddhist document to prove that Buddha or his first disciples had anything to do with [[mudrūs,|mudrās,]] mantras and [[mandalas|maṇḍalas]] and that neither Yuan Chwang nor I-tsing refers to any Tantras, though both of them refer to the Buddhist [[mona stories|monasteries]] as centres of Buddhist culture (vide Dr. De in N. I. [[a.|A.]] vol. I. pp. 1 ff). In the introduction to [[Sadhanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] (vol. II. [[LXVIII.)|p. LXVIII.)]] Dr. Bhattacharya relies upon the words [['Sugatom padistam'|'Sugatōpadiṣṭaṁ']] and 'Sugataiḥ' occurring on pp. 334–335 of the [[Sadhanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] for holding that Buddha himself must have promulgated some mantras. There are two weighty objections [[this|viz.]] 'Sugataiḥ' does not always mean Buddha, but means also 'followers of Buddha' and secondly, just as most Hindu Tantras are dialogues between Śiva and [[Parvati,|Pārvatī,]] so later Buddhist writers might have easily said that they [[aro|are]] quoting the Buddha; the same objection applies to [[Kamalaśila's romarks|Kamalaśīla's remarks]] quoted by Dr. Bhattacharya, [[43|as]] [[Kamalasila|Kamalaśīla]] and his teacher [[tourisbed|flourished]] about 1200 years after Buddha. 

The question whether Buddhist Tantras were prior to Hindu Tantras or _vice versa_ is difficult to decide. It appears probable that both arose nearly about the same time. Vide 'The [[Sāktas'|Śāktas']] by E. A. Payne pp. 72–74 for discussion of views. In the [[Sādbanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] (a Vajrayāna work consisting of 312 small works composed according to Dr. Bhattacharya from the 3rd century [[A, D.|A. D.]] to 12th century A. D.) four [[pithas|pīṭhas]] (chief centres) of Vajrayāna[^1673] are mentioned viz. Kāmākhyā, Sirihaṭṭa (or [[Srthatta ),|Śrīhaṭṭa ),]] Pūrṇagiri and [[Uddiyana.|Uḍḍiyāna.]] The first two are [[reaper|respec-]]

[^1673]: [[It appears that in some Tantra works five Pithas ara araad (according to A P. Sastri's Cat, of Nepal Palm-leaf and selected papar mss. in the Nepal Durbar Library. Calcotta, 1905, p. LXXX) viz. Odiyada (lu Orissa, says H. P. Sastri). Jāla (in Jalaodar). Pūrṇa Mataāga lo Srilaisi and Kāmākbyā in Assam. The very fact tbat five pitbas are named ia tbo work supposed to be delivered by Śiva shows beyond doubt that before the work Tantrism had spread in all parts of India. The Sadbanamālā (vol II pp. 453 aod 455) meations Uddiyana, Purnagiri, Kāmākhyā and Strihagga ; the Kulacūļāmagitantra (Tantrik texts, Vol. IV) in 6tb patala versos 3-7) refers to five piṭbas viz, Uddiyāna, Kanarūpe, Kāmākhyā, Jalandhara and (Continued on natt page)Pithas of Vajrayana 1039 (Continued from last page) Purnagiri (vide also 3rd patala, 59-61). In I. H. Q. vol. XI. pp 142-144 it la argued that Uddiyana and Sabore are in Bengal. The Devibhāgavata (VII, 30. 55-80) aames over one hondred kṣetras of Devi.|It appears that in some Tantra works five Pīṭhas are named (according to H. P. Sastri's Cat. of Nepal Palm-leaf and selected paper mss. in the Nepal Durbar Library, Calcutta, 1905, p. LXXX) viz. Oḍiyāna (in Orissa, says H. P. Sastri), Jāla (in Jalandar), Pūrṇa Matāṅga in Śrīśaila and Kāmākhyā in Assam. The very fact that five pīṭhas are named in the work supposed to be delivered by Śiva shows beyond doubt that before the work Tāntrism had spread in all parts of India. The Sādhanamālā (vol II pp. 453 and 455) mentions Uḍḍiyāna, Pūrṇagiri, Kāmākhyā and Sirihaṭṭa; the Kulacūḍāmaṇitantra (Tāntrik texts, Vol. IV) in 6th paṭala verses 3–7) refers to five pīṭhas viz. Uḍḍiyāna, Kāmarūpa, Kāmākhyā, Jālandhara and Pūrṇagiri (vide also 3rd paṭala, 59–61). In I. H. Q. vol. XI. pp 142–144 it is argued that Uḍḍiyāna and Sahore are in Bengal. The Devībhāgavata (VII. 30. 55–80) names over one hundred kṣetras of Devī.]]

[[P1039]]tively identified with [[Kamakhya or Kamarūpa|Kāmākhyā or Kāmarūpa]] ( three miles from Gauhati) and modern Sylhet. The exact situation of the other two is a controversial matter. M. M. [[E.|H.]] P. Shastri identified [[Uddiyāna|Uḍḍiyāna]] (which is most frequently mentioned as a [[pitha|pīṭha]] ) with Orissa. His son Dr. B. Bhattacharya thinks it most probable that Vajrayāna [[Tāntrioism|Tāntricism]] arose in [[Uddiyāna|Uḍḍiyāna]] (p. 46 of Intro. to B. E.). Dr. Bagchi in 'Studies in the Tantras' pp. 37-40 furnishes good grounds for holding that [[Uddiyāna|Uḍḍiyāna]] was near Swat [[valloy|valley]] in N. W. India and Grousset 'In the footsteps of Buddha' pp. 109-110 holds the same view. The [[Bārbaspatya Sūtra|Bārhaspatyasūtra]] (ed. by F. W. Thomas) names eight [[sāktaksetras|Śāktakṣetras]] (III. 123–124). In his Intro. to [[Bādhanamala|Sādhanamālā]] (vol. II. p. LXXVIII) Dr. Bhattacharya holds that the Hindu tantras were introduced on the model of the Buddhist [[tantrag.|tantras.]] But Winternitz (in 'History of Indian Literature', Eng. tr., vol. II. p. 401) states that this view of Dr. Bhattacharya is contrary to the facts and the present writer agrees with this view. 

Though Dr. Bhattacharya admits that Buddhism and Jainism exploited Hindu gods in the earlier period, he asserts (on p. 147 of his Intro. to B. E.) that 'it is possible to declare without fear of contradiction that the Buddhists were the first to introduce the Tantras in their religion and that the Hindus borrowed them from the Buddhists in later times'. It is no honour to ancient Hindus to be called the pioneers in the practice of black [[magio.|magic.]] But scholars have to seek truth irrespective of the question of honour or dishonour. Very weighty arguments are advanced by Valleé Poussin (in E. R. E. vol. XII, p. 193), Winternitz and Payne (on [['|'Śāktas]] p. 73 ) for the opposite view and the present author [[agroos|agrees]] with them. Hundreds of works were translated from Sanskrit into Tibetan and Chinese. The [[traffio|traffic]] of borrowing has been all one way from India to Tibet and China. Vide a paper on 'China's debt to India' by Prof. Liang Chi Chao in [[Viśvabhārati|Viśvabhāratī]] Quarterly, vol. II, for 1924-25 pp. 251–261, where it is stated that 24 Hindu scholars from A. D. 67 to 789 came to China, besides 13 from Kashmir and that the Chinese scholars that went to India for study from 265-790 A. D. numbered 187 of which the names of 105 can be ascertained. 

[[P1040]]There is hardly any evidence of the translations of Chinese or Tibetan works into Sanskrit. Besides, the three great Chinese travellers never refer to the study of Buddhist Tantras in India. Watters [[On|on]] 'Yuan Chwang's Travels in India ' vol. I. p. 360 [[narratos|narrates]] a story from the pilgrim's life that when he left Ayodhyā in a boat and proceeded east down the Ganges, thugs that looted the boat decided to sacrifice him to Durgā but that the Chinese pilgrim was saved by a hurricane which put the thugs in terror, who released him and treated him with [[Teveronce.|reverence.]] Vide also 'In the Footsteps of Buddha' by René Grousset pp. 133-135 for this incident. We see that there is evidence of the prevalence of Tāntrik and Śākta worship in India long before the 7th century A. D. There is hardly any evidence of any Buddhist Tāntrik work before 650 [[A, D.|A. D.]] except [[perbaps|perhaps]] the [[Guhyasamajatantra|Guhyasamājatantra]] and [[Manjusrimūlakalpa,|Mañjuśrīmūlakalpa,]] both of which contain late elements. In this way, presumption and chronology are both against borrowing by Hinduism from Buddhist Tibetan or Chinese Tāntrik works. Vide 'Tibet past and present' by Sir Charles Bell (1924) pp. 23, 25, 29, Sardar K. M. Panikkar's work 'India and China' (1957) p. 70, [['Intro duction|'Introduction]] of the alphabet in Tibet' by M. M. Dr. Satischandra who holds that it was borrowed from Magadha in 7th Century A. D., that show that a written script based on an Indian alphabet as prevalent in Kashmir was first introduced about 640 A. D., that the Tāntrik Buddhist Padmasambhava was summoned from [[Uddiyāna|Uḍḍiyāna]] by a Tibetan king [[Ti-son Do-tson|Ti-son De-tson]] (749-786 A. D.) on the recommendation of [[Santarakṣita|Śāntarakṣita]] Bodhisattva and was induced to settle in Tibet. Bunjiu Nanjio's 'Catalogue of [[Tripitaka'|Tripiṭaka']] (Oxford, 1883), appendix II. p. 445 No. 155 shows that Amoghavajra translated many works between 746 and 771 A. D., died in 774 A. D. and that it was under his influence that Tantra doctrines gained currency in China. It is clear from the works of Bāṇa as detailed later on that worship of [[Candikā|Caṇḍikā]] with [[wide|wine]] and flesh was prevalent in India long before 600 A. D., that [[Śriparvata|Śrīparvata]] was famed for its tāntrik siddhis, that [[sivasamhitas|Śivasaṁhitās]] existed, that japa of mantras a crore of times in a cemetery was [[gupposed|supposed]] to confer siddhis, that [[he|the]] 14th of the dark half of a month was deemed the proper tithi for japa and black magic. Therefore, it is most likely that [[Sākta|Śākta]] or Tāntrik [[dootrines|doctrines]] were taken to China and Tibet from India and not _vice versa_. Prof. P. V. Bapat in '2500 years of Buddhism' (pp. 360-376) follows (at p. 363) Dr. B. Bhattacharya and tries to prove that Tibetan Tāntrism is earlier than Hindu [[Tantrism,|Tāntrism,]] but his [[argumento|arguments]] 

[[P1041]]like those of Dr. Bhattacharya do not [[oarry|carry]] conviction at all. Dr. A. S. Altekar in his paper on Sanskrit Literature in Tibet ( ABORI. [[Vol.|vol.]] 35 pp. [[54-66|54–66]] ) shows how Buddhism entered into Tibet in the reign of [[Strong-Tsan-Gampo|Sroṅg-Tsan-Gampo]] ([[637-693 A, D.|637–693 A. D.]] ), that about 750 A. D. [[ |]]Padmasambhava from Orissa and Vairocana from Kashmir were pioneers and how about 4500 works were translated into Tibetan. 

Even Dr. Bhattacharya admits that Buddhist Tantras in outward appearance resemble in a marked degree Hindu Tantras (p. 47 of Intro. to B. E.), but he contends that in the subject [[inatter,|matter,]] the philosophical doctrines and religious principles there is little similarity. As Buddhism did not believe in Hindu gods they do not speak about [[Sakti or Sāktism.|Śakti or Śāktism.]] But just as in Hindu Tantras there is the male principle Śiva and the female principle of [[Devi,|Devī,]] the Buddhists postulate Prajñā (which is feminine) and Upāya (masculine) as two principles and invest them with the same roles as those of [[Siya and Davi but reyorsed|Śiva and Devī but reversed]] in character. They had to graft on the idea of [[Sunyata|Śūnyatā]] the ideas underlying the concepts of [[Siya and Devi or Sakti.|Śiva and Devī or Śakti.]] The subject matter is very similar as regards the goal and means ( Yoga [[&o|&c]] ) and the procedure of mantra, guru, [[mandala|maṇḍala]] &c. is the same. The most important and early works of Buddhist Tāntric cult, the [[Prajñopāya-viniscayasiddhi and Jõānasiddhi|Prajñopāya-viniścayasiddhi and Jñānasiddhi]] are not earlier than the 8th century A. D., when [[Sāktism and Tantrismo had both boon|Śāktism and Tāntrism had both been]] long established in India. 

The word '[[Sākta |Śākta]]' means one who is a worshipper or devotee of '[[Sakti'|Śakti]]' (cosmic power or energy ). It appears that long before the 8th century A. D. this cult had spread in almost all parts of India, particularly in Bengal and Assam. [[Sakti|Śakti]] under different names (such as Tripurā, [[Lohita, Sdaśikā, Kāmeśvari|Lohitā, Ṣoḍaśikā, Kāmeśvarī]] ) was conceived to be the primordial principle of all activity in the universe and is generally worshipped under the name of [[Devi.|Devī.]] The [[Devīmābātmya|Devīmāhātmya]] is one of the chief works of the [[Saktas|Śāktas]] and has been described above on pp. [[155-156.|155–156.]] The chief characteristics of the [[sākta|Śākta]] cult are the [[doctrinc|doctrine]] that God or Deity is one and is to be conceived as the mother and also the [[destroyor|destroyer]][^1674] and that there is a special form of ceremonial 

[^1674]: One of the _dhyānas_ of [[Devi as Kali|Devī as Kālī]] is as follows: [[I HERI घोरदंडीसम्मुनीम् । चर्सो समुण्ड-बरामयका शिवाम् । मुण्डमालाधरी देवी उलविहां दिमन्वयम् । एवं सञ्चिन्तयेकालीं श्मशानालयवासिनीम् । कालीमन्त्र in झारमोद 

(Vepk, ed.)|शवारूढां महाभीमां घोरदंष्ट्रां हसन्मुखीम् । चतुर्भुजां खड्गमुण्ड-वराभयकरां शिवाम् ॥ मुण्डमालाधरां देवीं ललज्जिह्वां दिगम्बराम् । एवं सञ्चिन्तयेत्कालीं श्मशानालयवासिनीम् ॥ कालीतन्त्र in शाक्तप्रमोद (Venk. ed.)]]

[[P1042]]worship which sometimes assumed debased and revolting forms. [[Dari|Devī]] has been eulogised in other Purāṇas also as in [[Vamana (18-19), Doyl-bhagavata (III. 27), Brahmānda (that oontains the Lalitāmāhātmaya|Vāmana (18–19), Devī-bhāgavata (III. 27), Brahmāṇḍa (that contains the Lalitāmāhātmya]] in 44 chapters), Matsya (13. [[24-54,|24–54,]] where 108 names of [[Devi|Devī]] and 108 places of her worship are set out), Kūrma (I. 12). In the last Purāṇa (Kūrma I. 12 ) [[Devi|Devī]] is called [[Mahamahiṣamardini|Mahāmahiṣamardinī]] (98), [[Anahata, Kundalini|Anāhatā, Kuṇḍalinī]] (128), Durgā, [[Katyayani, Candi, Bhadrakali|Kātyāyanī, Caṇḍī, Bhadrakālī]] (143 and 148 ) and it is stated that the śāstras opposed[^1675] to the Veda and [[Smrti|Smṛti]] that are popular among people such as Kāpāla, Bhairava, [[Yamala, Vāma, Arbata|Yāmala, Vāma, Ārhata]] were propagated by [[Devi|Devī]] for deluding the world and were based on ignorance. Vide also Brahmapurāṇa ([[181.48-52|181. 48–52]] ) for names of [[Devi|Devī]] and for the proposition that [[Devi|Devī]] when worshipped with offerings of wine, flesh and other edibles becomes pleased and grants men's desires. [[Bhadrakāli|Bhadrakālī]] is [[com. paratively|comparatively]] an ancient name. In the [[Sāó. Gr.(SBE|Śāṅ. Gṛ. (SBE]] vol. XXIX p. 86 ) it is provided that an offering is to be made to [[Sri|Śrī]] at the head of the bed on which the householder sleeps and at the foot of the bed to [[Bhadra-kāli,|Bhadra-kālī,]] while Manu provides that, in the daily [[baliharana,|baliharaṇa,]] bali ([[offering )|offering)]] is to be offered to [[Bhadrakali|Bhadrakālī]] in the south-west. 

The Tantras and [[Sākta|Śākta]] works have much in common, the main point of difference being that in the [[Sākta|Śākta]] cult [[Devi|Devī]] (or Śakti) is worshipped as the highest, while Tantras (which include also Buddhist and Jaina works) are not restricted to the worship of [[Devi or Sakti,|Devī or Śakti,]] but may be agnostic, Vedāntic or Sāṅkhya in their philosophical outlook. Dr. B. Bhattacharya (in Intro. to [[Gubyasamājatantra|Guhyasamājatantra]] p. XXXIV and in [[Intro, to Sadhananālā|Intro. to Sādhanamālā]] vol. II, p. XIX) states that, for a work to be called a real Tantra, there must be the element of [[Sakti|Śakti]] in it. But this is plainly rather an over-statement. The Vāyupurāṇa [[enume. rates|enumerates]] the [[Sākta|Śākta]] among six _darśanas_ ( philosophio points of view) as in note [^1675a] 

Even the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] speaks of the [[Saktis|Śaktis]] of the great gods of the Vedic pantheon. But the [[Sakti or Saktis|Śakti or Śaktis]] are of the God

[^1675a]: [[2. nag troopt a site stat pureen I arama sone enante Parvana 1 104, 16.|ब्राह्मं शैवं वैष्णवं च सौरं शाक्तं तथार्हतम् । षड्दर्शनानि चोक्तानि स्वभाव-नियतानि च ॥ 104. 16.]]

[[P1043]][[nimself|himself]] and not a separate creative principle and sometimes [[Sakti|Śakti]] is meant as part of the poet, [[priøst|priest]] or sacrificer ( as in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 31. 18, I. 83. 3, IV. [[22, 8,|22. 8,]] X. 25. 5). The word 'Śakti' occurs in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] about a dozen times in the singular as well as in the plural, five times with Indra,[^1676] once with Aśvins ([[Rg. II. 39.7),|Ṛg. II. 39. 7 ),]] twice with [[pitps (I 109.3,|pitṛs ( I. 109. 3,]] VI. [[75.9)|75. 9 )]] and once with gods in general (X. 88. 10, who are said to have created Agni with their powers). Sometimes, the word 'Māyā' is used with regard to Indra instead of the word Śakti. 'O Indra! I long for your great friendship and powers ([[saktih|śaktīḥ]] )...understand that you are our great protector; I proclaim your ancient and recent deeds, O Indra endowed with powers ([[Saktivah|Śaktīvaḥ]])!......; 'Indra assuming[^1677] many forms by his powers (māyābhiḥ) repairs ([[to many saorifices), ton|to many sacrifices), ten]] hundred horses are yoked to his chariot'. In these passages there is no question of [[worship ping sakti or faktis|worshipping śakti or śaktis]] of the god praised. The more frequent word, however, is [['Saci' ('sacibhiḥ'|'Śacī' ('śacībhiḥ']] occurring 36 times and [['sacyā'|'śacyā']] 12 times). The word [['Sacipati'|'Śacīpati']] (lord of [[Saci|Śacī]] or power) occurs sixteen times in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] and has been applied in all places to Indra, except once in [[Rg. VII, 67.5|Ṛg. VII. 67. 5]] (where it is applied to [[Asvins).|Aśvins).]] It cannot be said that in the [[Rgveda Saci'|Ṛgveda 'Śacī']] is the wife of Indra (as it is said in later mythology), since the plural is more frequent than [[Saoi|Śacī]] in the singular and since [[Aśving|Aśvins]] also are called [['sacipati'.|'Śacīpati']] once. Similarly, the word [['Sacīvaḥ|'Śacīvaḥ']] occurs eleven times, in nine of which it is addressed to Indra but it is once applied to Agni ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] III. 21. 4) and once to Soma ([[Rg. IX. 87.9).|Ṛg. IX. 87. 9).]] The ideas associated with the words [['. Sakti' and Saci'|'Śakti' and 'Śacī']] are those of creation, protection, valour, and bounty. In [[Rg. I 56. 4|Ṛg. I. 56. 4]] Indra's power is called [['Devi tayiṣi'|'Devī taviṣī']] but the word [[Saci|Śacī]] does not occur in that verse. There is a sublime hymn ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 125 ) of the power of Vāk ( speech), wherein [[Vak|Vāk]] is said to associate with Rudras, [[Adityas,|Ādityas,]] Vasus, all gods and is

[[P1044]]declared to support Mitra and Varuṇa, Indra and [[Agai|Agni]], the [[Asyins|Aśvins]], Soma, [[Tvaṣtr|Tvaṣṭṛ]], Pūṣan and Bhaga. [[Vak|Vāk]] is said to stretch the bow for Rudra in order that the destructive [[enerny|enemy]] of brahma (prayer or God Brahmā) may be killed, that Vāk stands [[occupy. ing|occupying]] all worlds and that her body touches [[leavon|heaven]], that it is beyond heaven and the earth, that Vāk stands so vast by its greatness'. Vāk becomes the principle of all energy. [[Acoording|According]] to the [[Nighanṭu|Nighaṇṭu]] (I. 11), menā, gnāḥ and [[saci|śacī]] are three of the 57 words meaning 'Vāk'. In Tai. S. V. [[1.7.2|1. 7. 2]] the metres are called [['.gnās'|'gnās']]. [[Rg. I. 164, 41|Ṛg. I. 164. 41]] is an [[enigmatio|enigmatic]] description of [[Vak|Vāk]] explained in Nirukta XI. 40. It should be noticed that, just as [[Dovi|Devī]] or [[Sakti|Śakti]] is associated with Śiva in later literature, so are [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]], Varuṇānī, [[Agnāyi|Agnāyī]], [[Rodasi|Rodasī]] associated with Indra, [[Varupa|Varuṇa]], [[Agai|Agni]] and Maruts respectively as wives. 'I invoke [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]],[^1678] [[Varupāni|Varuṇānī]] and [[Agnāyi|Agnāyī]] for my welfare and for [[drink iog|drinking]] Soma'; 'May the women, the wives of gods, partake of the offering, viz. [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]], [[Agnayi|Agnāyī]], the brilliant ([[wife)|(wife)]] of the [[Asyins|Aśvins]], [[Rodasi|Rodasī]]; may [[Varuṇāṇi|Varuṇānī]] listen (to our laud); may the goddesses partake (of offerings) at the time (appropriate) for women'. It must be said, however, that these goddesses ([[deviḥ|devīḥ]]) play a very subordinate part in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]]. No direct connection can be traced between these Vedic goddesses and the later [[concep tion|conception]] of [[Devi|Devī]] or [[Sakti|Śakti]]. [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]] is invoked for protection in [[Rg. I 22. 12|Ṛg. I. 22. 12]], II. 32. 8, V. 46. 8, X. 86. [[11-12|11–12]]. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] V. 46. 8 [[Indrāni|Indrāṇī]] and three others are called Devapatnīs and 'gnās'. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 61. 8 it is said that the gnās, the wives of gods, [[wovo|wove]], when Indra struck the demon [[Abi|Ahi]], a song of worship. The word 'gnā' occurs 20 times in the [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] in the nominative, objective, [[instru mental|instrumental]] and locative and is an Indo-European word for wife (Greek has it). Vide Nir. III. 21 [[whera|where]] 'menā' and 'gnā' occur. In the Kenopaniṣad Umā [[Haimavati|Haimavatī]] (daughter of Himavat) tells of Brahma to the gods Agni, [[Vayu|Vāyu]] and Indra (III. 12). In the Śvetāśvataropaniṣad it is said 'they ([[brahma Yādins|brahmavādins]]) endowed with meditation and Yoga saw Śakti (power) abiding as non-different from God and concealed (from [[com|com-]]

[[P1045]]prehension) by its own attributes (or by sattva, rajas and tamas).' The same [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]] (in VI. 8) speaks of Brahma as possessing the highest [[Sakti|Śakti]][^1679] in various forms and this text is quoted by Śaṅkarācārya on Vedāntasūtra II. 1. 24. In the [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] on Vedāntasūtra II. 1. 30 and in the sūtra itself Brahma is said to be endowed with all powers. Vide also [[Svetasva.|Śvetāśva.]] IV. 1. The [[Nārāyanopaniṣad|Nārāyaṇopaniṣad]] contains an invocation of [[Durgā-devi|Durgā-devī]][^1680] 'I approach as a refuge the blazing goddess Durgā, brilliant like fire, luminous owing to tapas, that is resorted to for (yielding) the rewards of religious actions; O goddess possessed of excellent [[mighti|might!]] adoration to your power'. [[Raghavabhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]][^1681] [[avera|avers]] that Tantra cult is based on Śruti as can be seen from the Upaniṣads called [[Rāmapūrvottara- tāpaniya|Rāmapūrvottara-tāpanīya]] and [[Nṭsimbapūr vottara-tāpaniya|Nṛsiṃhapūrvottara-tāpanīya]]. Similarly, Bhāskararāya in his commentary Setubandha on Vāmakeśvaratantra mentions several [[Upa niṣads|Upaniṣads]] as treating in detail of the bhakti of Mahātripura-sundarī and interprets [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] V. 47. [[4'catvari|4 'catvāri]] [[im'|īm']] as referring to [[Kadividya,|Kādividyā.]] But all these Upaniṣads seem to be purposely composed to bolster up the tantras that had come to be looked down upon and they

It is possible that such a vedānta doctrine of sakti might have suggested the later all-engrossing power of Sakti as the only deity or principle|ते ध्यानयोगानुगता अपश्यन्देवात्मशक्तिं स्वगुणैर्निगूढाम् । श्वेताश्व० I. 3; परास्य शक्तिर्विविधैव श्रूयते स्वाभाविकी ज्ञानबलक्रिया च॥ श्वेताश्व० VI. 8; सर्वोपेता च तद्दर्शनात् । वेदान्तसूत्र II. 1. 30, on which शङ्कर says 'एकस्यापि ब्रह्मणो विचित्रशक्ति-योगादुपपद्यते विचित्रो विकारप्रपञ्च इति' । But this is entirely different from the later Śākta doctrines. Here Brahma is said to be endowed with various Śaktis (not one), while Śakti among Śāktas is the female principle that is supreme. It is possible that such a vedānta doctrine of śakti might have suggested the later all-engrossing power of Śakti as the only deity or principle]]

[^1680]: [[तामनिवर्णी तपसा ज्वलन्तीं वैरोचनी कर्मफलेषु जुष्टाम् । दुर्गा देवीं शरणमहं प्रपथे सुतरसितरसे नमः । नारायणोपनिषद 2. 1.|तामग्निवर्णां तपसा ज्वलन्तीं वैरोचनीं कर्मफलेषु जुष्टाम् । दुर्गां देवीं शरणमहं प्रपद्ये सुतरसितरसे नमः । नारायणोपनिषद् 2. 1.]]

[^1681]: [[श्रुतिमूलकता तु रामपूर्वोत्तरतापनीय-चसिंहपूर्वोत्तरतापनीय-सौराधाक्षर शैव पञ्चाक्षरामिका च साक्षास्कृतिरुपलभ्यत एव । राघवभह on शारवातिलक p. 23 श्रीमहा त्रिपुरसुन्दर्याश्च गौणी भक्ति सेतिकर्तग्यताका निरूपथितुमेरपर्येण सुन्दरीतापनीपञ्चक भावनोपनिषत्कोलोपनिषद-एलोपनिषन्महोपनिषच्चेत्यादयो वेदशिरोभागा: प्रवृत्ताः। सेतुबन्ध on वामकेश्वरतन्त्र p. 43; तथा च बहुचैराम्नायते चत्वार ई विप्रति...अन्सान इति । अथ हि कादिविद्याया ईकारचतुष्टयवमाविशेषणहारोद्धारः कुतः। सेतुबन्ध p. 17. In शाकोपनिषद edited with the com. of उपनिषद्ब्रह्मयोगिन् by Pandit A. Mahadevasastri, Adyar, 1925, are printed त्रिपुरा, त्रिपुरातापिनी, देवी, बहुच, भावना, सरस्वतीरहस्य, सीता, सौभाग्यलक्ष्मी (the भावनोपनिषद् occurring on pp 68-73). In the पहुचोप निषद् (on P. 62) occurs the passage 'सैषा परा शक्ति सैषा शाम्भवी विद्या काविविति पा हादिविधेति वा सादिविधेति पा रहस्यमोमों पाचि प्रतिष्ठा।.' The com. explains: श्रीमत्पञ्चदशाक्षरीमविभक्ता कएईलही-प्रथमखण्डात्मना काविीवियति वा-हस कहीं . इति द्वितीयखण्डात्मना हाविषियति वा सकलनोंदति सादिविधा पा रहस्यम्।|श्रुतिमूलकता तु रामपूर्वोत्तरतापनीय-नृसिंहपूर्वोत्तरतापनीय-सौराष्टाक्षर-शैव-पञ्चाक्षरात्मिका च साक्षाच्छ्रुतिरूपलभ्यत एव । राघवभट्ट on शारदातिलक p. 2; श्रीमहात्रिपुरसुन्दर्याश्च गौणीं भक्तिं सेतिकर्तव्यताकां निरूपयितुमैकपर्येण सुन्दरीतापनीपञ्चकं भावनोपनिषत्कौलोपनिषद-गूह्योपनिषन्महोपनिषच्चेत्यादयो वेदशिरोभागाः प्रवृत्ताः । सेतुबन्ध on वामकेश्वरतन्त्र p. 4; तथा च बह्वृचैराम्नायते—चत्वार ईं बिभ्रति...अन्तान्-इति । अत्र हि कादिविद्याया ईकारचतुष्टयवत्ताविशेषणद्वारोद्धारः कृतः । सेतुबन्ध p. 17. In शाक्तोपनिषदः edited with the com. of उपनिषद्ब्रह्मयोगिन् by Pandit A. Mahādevāśāstrī, Adyar, 1925, are printed त्रिपुरा, त्रिपुरातापिनी, देवी, बह्वृच, भावना, सरस्वतीरहस्य, सीता, सौभाग्यलक्ष्मी (the भावनोपनिषद् occurring on pp 68–73). In the बह्वृचोपनिषद् (on p. 62) occurs the passage 'सैषा परा शक्तिः सैषा शाम्भवी विद्या कादिविद्येति वा हादिविद्येति वा सादिविद्येति वा रहस्यमोंमों वाचि प्रतिष्ठा।' The com. explains: श्रीमत्पञ्चदशाक्षरीमविभक्ता क ए ई ल ह्रीं-प्रथमखण्डात्मना कादिविद्येति वा-ह स क ह ल ह्रीं-इति द्वितीयखण्डात्मना हादिविद्येति वा स क ल ह्रीं-इति सादिविद्या वा रहस्यम् ।]]

[[P1046]]are mostly mentioned by late [[medievel|medieval]] writers like [[Raghaven bhatta|Rāghavabhaṭṭa]] and [[Bhaskararārya.|Bhāskararāya.]] There are two great hymns addressed to Durgā in the Mahābhārata,[^1682] viz. in [[Virātaparya|Virāṭaparva]] (chap. 6) by [[Yudhisthira|Yudhiṣṭhira]] and the other in [[Bhiṣmaparya|Bhīṣmaparva]] (chap. 23) by Arjuna, but they are discarded as apocryphal in the Poona [[oritical|critical]] edition. The Gangadhara stone Inscription of Viśvavarman of 480 of the Mālava era (424 A.D.) refers to [[Mātra|Mātṛs]] (Mother [[Goddesses )|Goddesses)]] and Tantra.[^1683] The [[Br, S.|Bṛ. S.]] 57. 56 mentions the groups of [[Mātpe.|Mātṛs.]] The [[Výddha-Hārita-smrti|Vṛddha-Hārīta-smṛti]] [[rocommonds|recommends]] that the householder should not enter places of [[Saira,|Śaiva,]] Bauddha, Skānda and [[Sākta|Śākta]][^1684] cults. The Viṣṇupurāṇa[^1685] (one of the earliest among extant Purāṇas) speaks of the whole world as that of Viṣṇu, who is the highest Brahma and is endowed with

[^1682]: [[In JRAS for 1906 pp. 355-362 B, C, Majumdar endeavours to show tbat the two hymns to Dorgā are late interpolations in the Mahā. bbārata, probably derived from practices of god-Aryan Sūdras in Oriya speaking bill tribes of Sambalpar. But he forgets that apart from other sources Kalidasa (not later than about 400 A, D.) speaks of Parvati as Umā, Aparnā, Dargā, Gauri, Bbavāni and Candi in his several works and also that Kālidāsa refers to the Ardbanārīśvara form of Śiva. In the last verse of the Sakuntala Kalidāsa speaks of Śiva as 'parigata-faktih' and thereby suggests that at least tbe germs of the later Sakti worsbip were not unknown in his times. Therefore, the worsbip of Durgā in her several aspects is older than 300 A. D. by at least a century or more; vide pp. 185-186 above.|In JRAS for 1906 pp. 355–362 B. C. Majumdar endeavours to show that the two hymns to Durgā are late interpolations in the Mahābhārata, probably derived from practices of non-Aryan Śūdras in Oriya-speaking hill tribes of Sambalpur. But he forgets that apart from other sources Kālidāsa (not later than about 400 A. D.) speaks of Pārvatī as Umā, Aparṇā, Durgā, Gaurī, Bhavānī and Caṇḍī in his several works and also that Kālidāsa refers to the Ardhanārīśvara form of Śiva. In the last verse of the Śākuntala Kālidāsa speaks of Śiva as 'parigata-śaktiḥ' and thereby suggests that at least the germs of the later Śakti worship were not unknown in his times. Therefore, the worship of Durgā in her several aspects is older than 300 A. D. by at least a century or more; vide pp. 185–186 above.]]

[^1683]: [[मानणां च प्रमुदितधनात्यर्थनिर्वादिनीनां तन्त्रोद्धत-प्रबलपवनोदावितम्भी arrangi in Gupta Inscription No 17 p. 72. The Terrina 57, 56 provides rules about images of Mātrs 'Argia: aerog: paradag s hug', the विष्णुधर्मोत्तरपुराण (ia 1. 226) mentions a large number of मातृs including काली and AETHIT (io all over 180). Vide a recent work on the Cult of the Motber Goddesses' by E. O. James (London, 1959), of which pp. 99-124 deal with India; 'Matsyendranātha and bis Yogini colt' by Dr. Karambalkar in L. H. Q. Vol. XXXI (for 1935 ) pp. 362–374, which show that Adinatha (siva Himself) was the guru of Matsyendradātba, who was himself gura of Gorakṣazātha, the former being called Laipa (io Tibet), one of the 84 Siddhas; vide Cuppingham's Arcbaeological Survey Report IX, for the temple of 64 Yoginis at Bheragbat and Tantrik calt ia Epigraphs' by Mr, B. P. Desai in J. O. R (Mad. ), Vol. 19 pp. 285-288.|मातृणां च प्रमुदितघनात्यर्थनिर्ह्लादिनीनां तन्त्रोद्भूत-प्रबलपवनोद्वर्तिताम्भो-निधीनाम् । in Gupta Inscription No 17 p. 72. The बृहत्संहिता 57. 56 provides rules about images of Mātṛs 'मातृगणः कर्तव्यः स्वनामदेवानुरूपकृतचिह्नः' ; the विष्णुधर्मोत्तरपुराण (in I. 226) mentions a large number of मातृs including काली and महाकाली (in all over 180). Vide a recent work on 'the Cult of the Mother Goddesses' by E. O. James (London, 1959), of which pp. 99–124 deal with India; 'Matsyendranātha and his Yoginī cult' by Dr. Karambelkar in I. H. Q. Vol. XXXI (for 1955) pp. 362–374, which show that Ādinātha (Śiva Himself) was the guru of Matsyendranātha, who was himself guru of Gorakṣanātha, the former being called Luipa (in Tibet), one of the 84 Siddhas; vide Cunningham's Archaeological Survey Report IX, for the temple of 64 Yoginīs at Bheraghat and 'Tāntrik cult in Epigraphs' by Mr. B. P. Desai in J. O. R (Mad.), Vol. 19 pp. 285–288.]]

[[P1047]][[Sakti|Śakti]], enumerates some of the names of Durgā as [[Ārya|Āryā]], Vedagarbhā, Ambikā, Bhadrā, [[Bhadra-kāli|Bhadra-kālī]], [[Ksomadā|Kṣemadā]], Bhāgyadā and winds up by stating that when Durgā is worshipped with offerings of wine, flesh, various kinds of foods, she, being pleased, would fulfil all desires of men. In the [[Kādambari|Kādambarī]] of [[Bāṇabhatta|Bāṇabhaṭṭa]] there is a long description of the temple of [[Candikā|Caṇḍikā]] at a few days' journey from [[Ujjayini,|Ujjayinī,]] where there was an old [[Dravida|Draviḍa]] devotee, in which the following points deserve to be noted, viz. offerings of the heads of animals, lion as vāhana, the slaughter of Mahiṣāsura, the doctrines of Pāśupatas written down on small books of palm leaves containing jugglery, tantra and mantras, Durgā-stotra written on a piece of cloth, ruined temples of the [[Mātrs|Mātṛs]] and description of the [[Dravida|Draviḍa]] devotee as knowing thousands of wonderful stories about [[Sriparvata.|Śrīparvata.]] [[Bāpa|Bāṇa]] describes at length what queen [[Vilāsavati,|Vilāsavatī,]] pining for a son, began doing to placate all Gods viz. sleeping in the shrines of [[Candikā|Caṇḍikā]] where guggulu was being incessantly burnt, taking auspicious baths on nights of dark 14th in public squares where magic circles had been drawn by great magicians, visiting temples of [[Mātra,|Mātṛs,]] wearing amulets inside which were pieces of birch leaves on which mantras had been written with yellow pigment, and, when delivery was [[dear|near]], her bed was rendered holy with various herbs, roots and [[yantrag|yantras]] (figures or diagrams). In the [[Harsacarita|Harṣacarita]] (III) there is a reference to magic circles and to human sacrifices in the description of the [[Saiya|Śaiva]] ascetic [[Bhairavā cārya|Bhairavācārya]] who had all the [[Śaiva-samhitas|Śaiva-saṃhitās]] by heart, who performed the japa of a mahāmantra called [[Mahākālahṭdaya &|Mahākālahṛdaya a]] crore of times in a cemetery and wanted the help of [[Puṣpabhāti|Puṣpabhūti]] (an [[ancostor|ancestor]] of Emperor [[Harsa )|Harṣa)]] for perfection in that mantra to be achieved by subduing a [[vetala|vetāla]] and who ultimately attained to the position of [[Vidyadhara|Vidyādhara]] and rose into the starry firmament. In the last [[Introductory verge|Introductory verse]] of the Harṣacarita the Emperor [[Harga|Harṣa]] is, by means of puns, called the Śrīparvata in yielding all Siddhis (supernatural powers, or fulfilment) according to the wishes of all [[supplianta.|suppliants.]][^1686] [[Those|These]] descriptions from the works of Bāṇa (first half of 7th century) show [[bow|how]] even long before the 7th century A. D. the worship of [[Candi|Caṇḍī]], with flesh and the [[Sakta|Śākta]] or Tāntrik paraphernalia of mantras, siddhis, [[mandalas|maṇḍalas]] and yantras had gripped the minds of all Indian people, great

[^1686]: [[जयति ज्वलसतापज्वलनमाकारकृतजगतमाः। सकलमणयिमनोरथ सिद्धिनीपर्वतो वर्ष हर्षचरित 1. 21; सौदामिनी says in मालतीमाधव 'गुरुचर्या वपस्तन्त्रमन्त्रयोगाभि orang I FAA Ftoff FARAT frarau: 1) IX.|जयति ज्वलत्प्रतापज्वलनप्राकारकृतजगद्रक्षः। सकलप्रणयिमनोरथ-सिद्धिः श्रीपर्वतो हर्षः ॥ हर्षचरित I. 21; सौदामिनी says in मालतीमाधव 'गुरुचर्या तपस्तन्त्रमन्त्रयोगाभियोगजाम् । इमामाक्षेपणीं सिद्धिमातनोमि शिवाय वः॥' IX.]]

[[P1048]]and small, rich and poor. In the [[Mālatimadhava (Aot|Mālatīmādhava (Act]] V) we have a gruesome picture of human sacrifice to [[Camunda.|Cāmuṇḍā.]] In the same drama [[Saudamini|Saudāminī]] is described as observing the vows of a Kāpālika on Śrīparvata and as having secured supernatural powers by means of mantras. [[Sriparvata|Śrīparvata]] is mentioned as a holy place of Śiva and [[Dovi|Devī]] in Vanaparva 85. [[19.-20.|19–20.]] The [[Vāsavam datta|Vāsavadattā]] of Subandhu (p. 87 of Hall's ed.) speaks of [[Sriparvata|Śrīparvata]] as 'Sannihita-mallikārjunaḥ.' Later on a few passages from Sanskrit and Prakrit literature will be cited to show how the teaching of Tāntrik practices led to [[grost|great]] moral debasement and revolting orgies in the name of religion.

The literature on Tantras was vast (vide 'Principles of Tantra' ed. by A. Avalon, part 1 pp. [[390-392|390–392]] for a long list of Tantras). Both Hindu and Buddhist writers composed numerous works on Tantra and a very large number of subjects came to be included in Tantra works. Buddhist and Hindu Tantras are alike in some respects but they differ in the topics discussed, philosophical doctrines and some religious principles and practices. Tāntra works were introduced into Tibet, Mongolia, China, Japan and South East Asia. Originals of many of the Sanskrit Tāntrik works are now not available, but the [[trang lations|translations]] of some of them in Tibetan are available.[^1687] It is [[aid|said]] that even now if proper search is made, three hundred works on Tantra may be discovered (vide Dr. B. Bhattacharya in vol. X. of Śrī [[Rāmavarma|Rāmavarmā]] Institute of Research at Cochin p. 81 ).

It is difficult to give a general definition of Tantras. The word 'Tantra' is derived usually from 'Tan' to spread and [['trai' (to saye).|'trai' (to save).]] 'It spreads (dilates upon) [[Dacy|many]] matters including the tattvas and mantras and affords protection;

[^1687]: [[The following works will convey some idea of Sakta doctrines and practices: R. G. Bhandarkars's 'Vaignavism, Saivism &c.' (in Collected Works, Vol. IV pp. 203-210); "Sakti and Sakta' by Sir Jobo Woodroffo (1920); 'Serpent Power' by Arthur Avalon (Sir Jobo Woodroffe); "the Sāktas' by E. A, Payne (Oxford University Press, 1933) Sakti or divine power' by Dr. Sudhendu Kumar Das (Calcutta University, 1945); 'Doctrine of Sakti in lodian Literatore' by Dr. P. C Chakravarti (1940). Vide Prof. Bagchi's 'Studies in the Tantras. pp 1-3 for the introduction of four Tāatrit texts in Kamboja (Cambodia) about 800 A, D, pamed Sirascheda, Vināsikba, Sammoba and Nayottara and Inscriptions from Kambaja' by Dr. R. C. Majumdar (Calcutta, 1953) pp. 362, 373–374 and JRAS for 1950 pp. 163-65 for relics of Sāktiem lo Moslem Malaya.Description and contents of Tantras|The following works will convey some idea of Śākta doctrines and practices: R. G. Bhandarkar's 'Vaiṣṇavism, Śaivism &c.' (in Collected Works, Vol. IV pp. 203–210); 'Śakti and Śākta' by Sir John Woodroffe (1920); 'Serpent Power' by Arthur Avalon (Sir John Woodroffe); 'the Śāktas' by E. A. Payne (Oxford University Press, 1933); 'Śakti or divine power' by Dr. Sudhendu Kumar Das (Calcutta University, 1945); 'Doctrine of Śakti in Indian Literature' by Dr. P. C Chakravarti (1940). Vide Prof. Bagchi's 'Studies in the Tantras' pp 1–3 for the introduction of four Tāntrik texts in Kambuja (Cambodia) about 800 A. D. named 'Śiraścheda, Vināśikha, Sammoha and Nayottara and 'Inscriptions from Kambuja' by Dr. R. C. Majumdar (Calcutta, 1953) pp. 362, 373–374 and JRAS for 1950 pp. 163–65 for relics of Śāktism in Moslem Malaya.]]

[[P1049]]therefore it is called Tantra '.[^1688] They can only be [[desoribed|described]] but one matter is included in almost all of them, [[vig,|viz.]] the five 'Makāras'. Often they contain an amalgam of religion, philosophy, superstitious dogmas, rites, astronomy, astrology, medicine, prognostications. They [[resomble|resemble]] the Purāṇas in several respects. The Buddhists deified many important [[per sonages|personages]] of Buddhism and took over in course of time certain Hindu deities like Gaṇeśa and [[Sarasvati.|Sarasvatī.]] Tantras are classified by comparatively later works into three groups, Viṣṇukrānta, Rathakrānta and Aśvakrānta and 64 Tantras are assigned to each of the three groups (vide Tāntrik Texts, vol. I. ed. by Arthur Avalon, Introduction pp. II–IV); but these numbers seem to be fictitious. The same Tantra is put in two classes by certain works. The Kulārṇava-tantra (III. 6–7) speaks of five [[ūmnāyas|āmnāyas]] (East, West, South, North and ūrdhva) as the paths to [[Moksa.|Mokṣa.]] The Paraśurāma-kalpasūtra[^1689] I. [[%|2]] does the same. Besides, Tāntrik worshippers are divided into three classes viz. Śaiva, Śākta and Vaiṣṇava. Bagchi states ('Studies in [[Tantras'p.|Tantras' p.]] 3) that Tāntrik literature is classified into Srotas (which are three) [[Pitha|Pīṭha]] and [[Amnāya.|Āmnāya.]] The Saundaryalaharī, ascribed by some to the great Advaita teacher Śaṅkarācārya, refers to sixty-four Tantras (in verse 31 which begins [['catuṣṣaṣtyā tantraih'|'catuṣṣaṣtyā tantraiḥ']] which, it is said therein, were declared by God [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] for deluding[^1690] the world. Several Hindu and Buddhist Tantras

Bose on "Tantras, their philosophy and occult secrets' pp 29–30 for the dames of 64 tantras men tioned in the Vārāhi-tantra together with the verses thereia and Saundarya. labari (tr. pp. 117–120) for a list of 64 tantras and Bagchi's 'Stadies in the Tantras' p. 5 for the names of tantras considered authoritative in the 8th century A.D, and even prior to it. The areas of s elf states that there are groups of ten, 18 and 64Salva tantras aterestre ento Teor fuit: 1 ami fanare HR Arnare u I. 18 (Kasbmir S. series, Vol. XXII p. 35). The facut terrenruta (a part of the Sea ) games the 64 Tantras in varios 13–22 of the first a n, but it includes eight Yamalas among Tantras, whila Dr. Bhattacharya (in Introduction to E, B. p. 52) tries to distinguish Agamas and Yāmalas from Tantra and also in his Intro. to Sadhanamilā Vol. II. pp. XXI-XXII. The m ore of a fin (Tiptrik Texts, vol. XIV) epomerateg numerous tantras iacluding Yamalas lo 1. 2-14 and names (1.92-93) eight gorus.|The evidence for attributing the work Saundaryalaharī to the great ācārya is not strong. In H. P. Sāstrī's Cat. of Palm-leaf mss. in Nepa Darbar Library p. LXII there is an entry for तारारहस्यवृत्तिका, a Tāntrik compilation by a Śaṅkarācārya of Gauḍadeśa. This would emphasize that caution is required in accepting works ascribed to Śaṅkarācārya as genuine works of the great Advaita teacher. Vide D. N. Bose on 'Tantras, their philosophy and occult secrets' pp 29–30 for the names of 64 tantras mentioned in the Vārāhī-tantra together with the verses therein and Saundaryalaharī (tr. pp. 117–120) for a list of 64 tantras and Bagchi's 'Studies in the Tantras' p. 5 for the names of tantras considered authoritative in the 8th century A.D. and even prior to it. The तन्त्रालोक of अभिनवगुप्त states that there are groups of ten, 18 and 64 Śaiva tantras 'दशाष्टादशधा भिन्नं यच्छासनं विभोः । तत्सारं त्रिकशास्त्रं हि तत्सारं मालिनीमतम् ॥' I. 18 (Kashmir S. series, Vol. XXII p. 35). The नित्याषोडशिकार्णव (a part of वामकेश्वरतन्त्र) names the 64 Tantras in verses 13–22 of the first विश्राम, but it includes eight Yāmalas among Tantras, while Dr. Bhattacharya (in 'Introduction to E. B. p. 52) tries to distinguish Āgamas and Yāmalas from Tantra and also in his Intro. to Sādhanamālā Vol. II. pp. XXI–XXII. The कौलावलीनिर्णय of ज्ञानानन्दगिरि (Tāntrik Texts, vol. XIV) enumerates numerous tantras including Yāmalas in I. 2–14 and names (I. 92–93) eight gurus.]]

[[P1050]]have been published and we have now a fair idea of what the numerous Tantras must have been like. Some of the published Hindu Tantras are Kulārṇava, Tantrasāra, Nityotsava, [[Parasuramakalpa-sūtra,|Paraśurāmakalpa-sūtra,]] Pārānandasūtra, [[Prapancasāra,|Prapañcasāra,]] [[Mantra mahodadhi|Mantramahodadhi]] of [[Mahidhara,|Mahīdhara,]] Mahānirvāṇatantra, Rudrayāmala, Vāmakeśvaratantra, Śāradātilaka (about 11th century A. D.). Besides, there are works like the Tantrāloka and [[Malinlvijaya vārtika|Mālinīvijayavārtika]] of [[Abhinava-gupta|Abhinavagupta]] of Kashmir [[Tantrism.|Tāntrism.]] They stand somewhat apart from the works enumerated above. Among the published Buddhist tantras [[are;|are:]] Advayavajrasaṅgraha, [[Aryaman juśrimūlakalpa,|Āryamañjuśrīmūlakalpa,]] [[Guhayasamāja-tantra|Guhyasamāja-tantra]] (probably 6th century A. D.), Jñānasiddhi of Indrabhūti (717 A. D.), [[Nispan. nayogāvali|Niṣpannayogāvalī]] of Abhayākaragupta (composed between the last quarter of 11th and first of 12th century A. D.), [[Prajñopāya viniscaya-siddhi|Prajñopāyaviniścaya-siddhi]] of [[Anangavajra ( about|Anaṅgavajra (about]] 705 A. D.), [[Ṣat-cakra nirūpaña|Ṣaṭ-cakranirūpaṇa]] (1577 A.D.), [[Sadhanamala|Sādhanamālā]] (containing 312 small works supposed to be from 3rd to 12th century A. D.). Of the Buddhist Tantras [[Aryamañjusrimūlakalpa|Āryamañjuśrīmūlakalpa]] and Guhyasamāja tantra[^1691] are the oldest according to Dr. B. Bhattacharya (Intro. p. [[XXXVIII,|XXXVIII.]] to Guhya-samājatantra). Most of the above works have been published by Arthur Avalon ([[Sir.|Sir]] John Woodroffe) and in the Gaikwad Oriental Series. Some of the Hindu Tantras contain sublime philosophical views derived from the Upaniṣads and the Gītā or from the Sāṅkhya and Yoga and the final goal according to them is [[Mukli|Mukti]] (liberation from the

[^1691]: [[Dr. Bhattacharya in Iatro, to Gabyasa māja p. XXXIV holds that Asanga is probably the author of the Gobyasamāja and therefore that work belongs to the 3rd or 4th century A, D, One has only to read the Maba. yānasātrālaikāra of Asanga ed, by Sylvain Levi and compare its refined and correct Sanskrit with the rather barbarous Sanskrit of the Gohyasamāja to come to the conclusion that the latter is not Asanga's work. There is no evidence to prove that Gobyasamāja belongs to the 3rd or 4th century A.D. It is probably two or more ceaturies later than that; Bagcbl (Studios in Tantras' p. 41) is against identifying Agaiga, the author of Sadhana No. 159, with the great teacher of Yogācāra,|Dr. Bhattacharya in Intro. to Guhyasamāja p. XXXIV holds that Asaṅga is probably the author of the Guhyasamāja and therefore that work belongs to the 3rd or 4th century A. D. One has only to read the Mahāyānasūtrālaṅkāra of Asaṅga ed. by Sylvain Levi and compare its refined and correct Sanskrit with the rather barbarous Sanskrit of the Guhyasamāja to come to the conclusion that the latter is not Asaṅga's work. There is no evidence to prove that Guhyasamāja belongs to the 3rd or 4th century A.D. It is probably two or more centuries later than that; Bagchi ('Studies in Tantras' p. 41) is against identifying Asaṅga, the author of Sādhana No. 159, with the great teacher of Yogācāra.]] 

[[P1051]]cycle of births and deaths) for all [[mon|men]] but to be secured by following the path laid down by the Tantras. As the number of published Hindu Tantras is considerable, reference will be made mainly to a few viz. Kulārṇava, Pārānandasūtra, [[Prapancasāra,|Prapañcasāra,]] [[Māhānirvāṇatantra,|Mahānirvāṇatantra,]] [[Vāmakeśyaratantra|Vāmakeśvaratantra]] ([[Anan.|Ānan.]] ed.), [[Sakti. sangamatantra,|Śaktisaṅgamatantra,]] [[Sāradātilaka|Śāradātilaka]] and in the case of the Buddhist tantras to [[Aryamañjuśrīmūla-kalpa,|Āryamañjuśrīmūla-kalpa,]] [[Guhyasa mājatantra,|Guhyasamājatantra,]] [[Prajõopāyaviniscayasiddhi,|Prajñopāyaviniścayasiddhi,]] [[Jhānasiddhi,|Jñānasiddhi,]] [[Sadhanamāla,|Sādhanamālā,]] [[Sekodde satikā.|Sekoddeśaṭīkā.]] The purpose of most Buddhist Tantras is to indicate a short path for attaining [[Buddha-hood|Buddhahood]] through Yoga practices and they introduce the element of [[Sakti|Śakti]] for [[Yogio|Yogic]] practices and for securing miraculous powers (called siddhis). In the History of Dharmaśāstra not much need be said about Buddhist Tantras except for comparison and stress will have to be laid on the Hindu Tantras alone. The philosophical aspects of Tāntrik culture may be studied in the Paraśurāmakalpasūtra, the [[Vāmakesvara-tantra,|Vāmakeśvara-tantra,]] Tantrarāja, the works on Kashmir [[Saivism,|Śaivism,]] works of [[Bhaskararāya,|Bhāskararāya,]] Bhāvanopaniṣad. This last is a late work dignified with the title of Upaniṣad, as it deals with bhāvanā and summarises the [[Vāsanāpatala|Vāsanāpaṭala]] of Tantrarājatantra (vide [[Intro,|Intro.]] to the latter p. 3). There are also Vaiṣṇava [[Tantras|Tāntras]] like the [[Gautamiya-tantra|Gautamīya-tantra]] (D. C. ms. No. 1120 of 1886-1892) and [[Kramadipikā|Kramadīpikā]] of [[Kesava|Keśava]] (who was a successor of Nimbārka) with the commentary of Govinda Vidyāvinoda (published in the Chowkhamba S. series), which are not referred to in this work from considerations of space. Vide Agnipurāṇa 39. 1-7 for the names of twenty-five [[Vaiṣnava|Vaiṣṇava]] Tantras dealing with the establishment of Viṣṇu image and other matters and [[Māheśvara tantra|Māheśvaratantra]] 26. 16-20. 

The Hindu tantras which are supposed to have embodied [[dia logues|dialogues]] between Śiva and [[Devi|Devī]] or Skanda or Bhairava and rarely others as in [[Dattatreyatantra|Dattātreyatantra]] (D. C. ms. No. [[96%|962]] of 1887-91) endeavour to show that they [[bane|base]] themselves on the Vedas, Āgamas, Smṛtis and Purāṇas, that there is an easier and quicker way to the final goal of [[moksa|mokṣa]] and they often quote Vedic [[Verses.|verses.]] For example, in the [[Kulārnava,|Kulārṇava,]] Śiva says to [[Devi|Devī]] 'I churned the great ocean of Vedas and [[Againas|Āgamas]] with the churning handle of (correct) knowledge. I [[know|knew]] the essence of these and took out the Kuladharma,[^1692] that the Kaulaśāstras are [[autho.|autho-]]

[^1692]: [[मथिला शानदण्डेन बेदागममहार्णवम् । सारझेन भया देवि कुलधर्मः समदुतः। furofeo|मथित्वा ज्ञानदण्डेन वेदागममहार्णवम् । सारज्ञेन मया देवि कुलधर्मः समुद्धृतः॥ कुलार्णव०]] II. 10; the [[greater|पारानन्दसूत्र]] III. 64 is almost the same [['ATUR TAPIS|'मथित्वा ज्ञानमन्थेन वेदागममहार्णवम् । पारानन्दमतं]] [[शुद्ध|शुद्धं]] [[रसज्ञेन मयोघृतम्॥ इति।|रसज्ञेन मयोद्धृतम्॥ इति।]] (p. [[7):|7);]] '[[कुलशामाणि|कुलशास्त्राणि]] सर्वाणि मयैवोक्तानि [[पावति|पार्वति]] । प्रमाणानि न सन्देहो न हन्तव्यानि हेतुभिः॥ [[देवताम्यः पितृम्यश्च|देवताभ्यः पितृभ्यश्च]] [[मधुबाता|मधुवाता]] ऋतायते। स्वादिष्टया [[भविश्या|मदिष्ठया]] क्षीरं [[सपिर्मधूदकम्|सर्पिर्मधूदकम्]] । हिरण्यपावाः [[खादिश्च|खादिरश्च]] अवानन् [[पुरुष पशुम्|पुरुषं पशुम्]] । दीक्षामुपेयादित्याद्याः प्रमाणं श्रुतयः [[पिये।|प्रिये।]] कुलार्णव II. 139-141. [[देवताग्य:|देवताभ्यः]] पितृभ्यश्च वायुपु. 74. 15, मधु...यते is Rg. I. [[90.6,|90. 6,]] स्वादि...या is Rg. [[Ix.1.1,|IX. 1. 1,]] क्षीर...दकम् is Rg. [[IX.67,32;|IX. 67, 32;]] हिरण्यपावाः is in Rg. [[Ix. 86.43.|IX. 86. 43.]] Most of the Vedic references are cleverly chosen to suggest sweet wine and flesh.

[[P1052]]ritative like Vedic texts and should not be nullified by [[ratiooina tion.|ratiocination.]] The same Tantra further asserts 'one who has studied the four Vedas but is ignorant of Kuladharma is inferior to [[& candāla,|a cāṇḍāla,]] while a [[capdala|cāṇḍāla]] who knows Kuladharmas is superior to a brāhmaṇa. If all dharmas such as sacrifices, pilgrimages and vratas are put on one side and Kuladharma on another side, Kaula (dharma) is superior.'[^1693] It is, therefore necessary to understand what is meant by Kula or Kauladharma. The [[Guhya-samaja 169t|Guhya-samāja]][^1694] states that Guhya means the three viz. body, speech and mind and 'samaja' means 'coming together', that Kula may comprehend five matters or three or 101 and that Guhya (as defined) is trikula.' God [[Śhaṅkara 16s deolared|Śaṅkara]][^1695] declared five tattvas, viz. wine, flesh, fish, mudra (hand and finger poses or the woman helper of a yogin) and sexual intercourse, that are [[aots|acts]] that become the means for the attainment of the position of a vira and that the mantra of Sakti does not confer perfection unless one follows the practices of Kula; therefore a person

[^1693]: एकतः सकला धर्मा [[यज्ञतीर्थवसादयः।|यज्ञतीर्थव्रतादयः।]] एकतः कुलधर्मश्च तत्र [[कोलोऽधिक:|कौलोऽधिकः]] [[पिये।|प्रिये॥]] चतुर्वेदी कुलाज्ञानी श्वपचादधमः [[मिये ।|प्रिये ।]] श्वपचोऽपि कुलज्ञानी [[ब्राह्मणादतिरिच्यते।|ब्राह्मणादतिरिच्यते ॥]] [[कुलार्णव तन्त्र|कुलार्णव-तन्त्र]] II. [[ll|11]] and 67. Vide महानिर्वाणतन्त्र IV. 42 for almost the same words.

[^1694]: [[विविध कायवाचितं गुणमित्यभिधीयते। समाज मीलनं प्रोक्तं सदाभि धानकम्।|त्रिविधं कायवाक्चित्तं गुह्यमित्यभिधीयते। समाजं मीलनं प्रोक्तं सर्वबुद्धाभि-धानकम् ॥]] [[एपसमाज|गुह्यसमाज]] 18th पटल p. 152; [[पम|पञ्चकं]] त्रिकुलं चैव [[स्वभावकशत|स्वभावैकशतं]] कुलम् ।... [[तवं|तत्त्वं]] पञ्चकुलं प्रोक्तं त्रिकुलं [[यामुच्यते|चाधुनोच्यते]] । ibid. p. 153.

[^1695]: [[वीरसाधनकर्माणि पञ्चतस्वोदितानि च। मधे मांस तथा मत्स्यहनानैथुनमेव च। पतानि पश्च तस्यानि खया प्रोक्तानि शङ्कररा महानिर्वाण I. 37; साधकs are of three kind. पा, वीर and दिग्य. vide शक्तिसङ्गमतन्त्र, कालीखण्ड VI. 21, महानिर्वाण I.51 and 55. IV. 18-19, कोलावलीनिर्णय VII. 186. कुलाचारं विना देवि शक्तिमन्त्री न सिद्धियः । तस्माकुलाचाररतः साधयेच्छक्तिसाधनम्। मयं मांस तथा मत्स्यं मुदा मैथुनमेव च । शक्तिपूजा: विधावाये पञ्चतत्त्वं प्रकीर्तितम् ॥ महानिर्वाण V.21-22. आये is vocative of आधा applied to शक्ति, the spouse of शिव. The कोलावलीनिर्णय states चणिका पूजयेद्यस्तु विना पशमकारकैः। चत्वारि तस्य नश्यन्ति आयुर्विद्या यशोधनम् ॥ मयं मांसं...मैथुनमेव च।... मकारपश्चकं देवि देवताप्रीतिदायकम् ॥...विना पञ्चोपचारं हि देवीपूजां करोति यः। योगिनीनो भवेब्रक्ष्यः पापं चैव पदे पदे॥ IV. 24-28; besides, कौलावलीनिर्णय II. 101-105 are moreatriking: संस्थाप्य वामभागेतु शक्ति स्वामिपरायणाम्।...विना शक्त्या या पूजा विफला मात्र संशयः। तस्माच्यक्तियुतो पीरो भवेश्च यत्नपूर्वकम् । या शक्ति सा महादेवी हररूपस्त साधकः अयोग्यचिन्तनाम्चैव देवस्वमुपजायते।...शक्तिविनापि पूजायां माधिकारी भवेचना.|वीरसाधनकर्माणि पञ्चतत्त्वोदितानि च। मद्यं मांसं तथा मत्स्यमुद्रामैथुनमेव च। एतानि पञ्च तत्त्वानि त्वया प्रोक्तानि शङ्कर ॥ महानिर्वाण I. 57; साधकs are of three kinds पशु, वीर and दिव्य. Vide शक्तिसङ्गमतन्त्र, कालीखण्ड VI. 21, महानिर्वाण I. 51 and 55, IV. 18-19, कौलावलीनिर्णय VII. 186. कुलाचारं विना देवि शक्तिमन्त्रो न सिद्धिदः । तस्मात्कुलाचाररतः साधयेच्छक्तिसाधनम् । मद्यं मांसं तथा मत्स्यं मुद्रां मैथुनमेव च । शक्तिपूजा-विधावाद्ये पञ्चतत्त्वं प्रकीर्तितम् ॥ महानिर्वाण० V. 21-22. आद्ये is vocative of आद्या applied to शक्ति, the spouse of शिव. The कौलावलीनिर्णय states चण्डिकां पूजयेद्यस्तु विना पञ्चमकारकैः। चत्वारि तस्य नश्यन्ति आयुर्विद्या यशोधनम् ॥ मद्यं मांसं...मैथुनमेव च।... मकारपञ्चकं देवि देवताप्रीतिदायकम् ॥...विना पञ्चोपचारं हि देवीपूजां करोति यः। योगिनीनां भवेद्भक्ष्यः पापं चैव पदे पदे॥ IV. 24-28; besides, कौलावलीनिर्णय II. 101-105 are more striking: संस्थाप्य वामभागे तु शक्तिं स्वामिपरायणाम् ।...विना शक्त्या तु या पूजा विफला नात्र संशयः। तस्माच्छक्तियुतो वीरो भवेच्च यत्नपूर्वकम् । या शक्तिः सा महादेवी हररूपस्तु साधकः । अन्योन्यचिन्तनाच्चैव देवत्वमुपजायते।...शक्तिं विनापि पूजायां नाधिकारी भवेत्क्वचित् ॥]]

[[P1053]]should be devoted to the Kula practices whereby he would attain to the [[sadhana|sādhanā]] of [[Sakti;|Śakti;]] wine, flesh, fish, mudra and sexual intercourse—these are declared to be the five tattvas in the procedure of the worship of [[Sakti.|Śakti.]] In another place the [[Mahanirvana|Mahānirvāṇa]][^1696] says that the individual soul, [[prakrti,|prakṛti,]] space, time, [[alkasa,|ākāśa,]] earth, water, fire and [[Vayu-these|Vāyu—these]] are called 'Kula' and that way of life whereby one looks upon all these as brahma without distinction is called Kulācāra, that confers the four goals viz. dharma, artha, kāma and mokṣa. The [[Śaktisangama tantra|Śaktisaṅgama-tantra]] states that Kula means the upāsakas ([[worshippers )|worshippers)]][^1697] of [[Kāli.|Kālī.]] The Kulārṇava states [[*Kula|'Kula]] means gotra and that springs from [[Sakti|Śakti]] and Śiva; that man is called Kaulika who knows that [[mokea|mokṣa]] is secured from that (i. e. [[Sakti|Śakti]] and Śiva). Śiva is called 'Akula' and [[Sakti|Śakti]] is called 'Kula'; those who contemplate on Kula and Akula are the wise kaulikas." Various other definitions are given in Guhyasamāja ([[Ist|1st]] [[patala|paṭala]] p. 6), in the Preface p. VIII of the [[Saktisangama-tantra, Tarakhanda|Śaktisaṅgama-tantra, Tārākhaṇḍa.]] But the same Tantra clinches the matter by declaring that "Śakti is known as Kula; her worship and the like are described ; that should be known as 'Kulācāra', which is difficult of attain-
ment even for gods. Worship, done with these alone viz. wine, flesh, fish, mudrā and sexual intercourse, is known as Kulācāra." The Pārānanda-sūtra[^1698] provides that the highest self is one,

[^1696]: जीवः प्रकृतितत्त्वं च [[विकालाकाशमेव|दिक्कालाकाशमेव]] च । [[क्षित्यतेजोवायवश्व|क्षित्यप्तेजोवायवश्च]] [[कुलमित्यभिधीयते।|कुलमित्यभिधीयते ॥]] [[ब्रह्मबुद्धच्चा|ब्रह्मबुद्ध्या]] निर्विकल्पमेतेष्वाचरणं च यत्। [[कुलाचारः स एवाये धर्मकामार्थमोक्षदः।|कुलाचारः स एवाद्ये धर्मकामार्थमोक्षदः ॥]] [[महानिर्वाण|महानिर्वाण०]] VII. 97-98. [[ID|In]] VII. 109-110 it identifies the five [[तस्व,|तत्त्व,]] मद्य, मांस, मत्स्य, मुद्रा and मैथुन with the five elements viz. तेजः (अग्नि), पवन, आपः, पृथिवी and वियत्.

[^1697]: श्रीकाल्युपासका ये च तत्फलं परिकीर्तितम् । [[तेषां समूहोदेवेशि कुलं सङ्कीर्तित भया॥|तेषां समूहो देवेशि कुलं सङ्कीर्तितं मया ॥]] [[शक्तिसहम,|शक्तिसङ्गम,]] कालीखण्ड chap. 3. 32; [[मयं मांस तथा मत्स्य मुद्रा मैथुनमेव च। एभिरेव कृता पूजा कुलाचारः प्रकीर्तितः । शक्तिसङ्गमा,|मद्यं मांसं तथा मत्स्यं मुद्रा मैथुनमेव च । एभिरेव कृता पूजा कुलाचारः प्रकीर्तितः ॥ शक्तिसङ्गम०,]] ताराखण्ड, 36th पटल, verses 18-20; कुलं गोत्रं समाख्यातं तच्च [[शक्तिशिवोद्भवम्।|शक्तिशिवोद्भवम् ।]] येन मोक्ष इति [[ज्ञान कोलिका सोभि. धीयते॥|ज्ञानं कौलिकः सोऽभि-धीयते ॥]] [[अकुल|अकुलं]] शिव इत्युक्तं कुलं शक्तिः प्रकीर्तिता। [[कुलाकुलानुसन्धानानिपुणाः कौलिका मिये।|कुलाकुलानुसन्धाननिपुणाः कौलिकाः प्रिये ॥]] कुलार्णव 17. 26-27.
