The drama was written between 1172-1175 A, D.|In मोहराजपराजय (G. O. S.) p. 100 the कौल says 'खाद्यते मांसमनुदिनं पीयते मद्यं च मुक्तसङ्कल्पम् । अनिवारितमनःप्रसर एष धर्मो मया दृष्टः ॥' (Sanskrit rendering of a Prākrit verse). The drama was written between 1172–1175 A. D.]]

[^1744]: [[अन्तःकौलो बहिःशैव लोकाचारे तु वैदिकम् । सारमादाय तिष्ठेत्तु नारिकेलफलं पया ॥ अपरार्क p. 10. I prefer the reading of one the mas. Doted in the foot dotes. The printed text reads अन्तःशैव बहिःकौलं लोकाचारे etc. The coooannt fruit prosents three aspects, first there is the hard outside shell, then there is the soft and tasty kernel inside the hard shell and thirdly there is water in addition to the soft kernel. The कुलार्णवतन्त्र has 'अन्तःकौलो बहिःशैव जनमध्ये तु वैष्णवः । कौलं गोपायेद्वेवि नारिकेलफलाम्बुवत् ॥ XI. 83. One ms, reads अन्तःकौला बहिःशैवाः सभायां वैष्णवा मताः and this last is often quoted, The कौलावलीनिर्णय (X. 84-95) reads अन्तःशाक्ता बहिःशैवाः...मताः । नानावेशधराः कौला विचरन्ति महीतले ॥ The sect mark of both Saivas and Sāktas is tripuṇḍra (three parallel lines of holy ashes on the forehead from one eye to the other, drawn with three Angers other than the thumb and small finger ). Vide बृहज्जाबालोपनिषद् IV. 10-11, देवीभागवत XI. 15. 17-23.|अन्तःकौलो बहिःशैव लोकाचारे तु वैदिकम्। सारमादाय तिष्ठेत्तु नारिकेलफलं यथा ॥ अपरार्क p. 10. I prefer the reading of one the mss. noted in the foot notes. The printed text reads अन्तःशैव बहिःकौलं लोकाचारे etc. The cocoanut fruit presents three aspects, first there is the hard outside shell, then there is the soft and tasty kernel inside the hard shell and thirdly there is water in addition to the soft kernel. The कुलार्णवतन्त्र has 'अन्तःकौलो बहिःशैव जनमध्ये तु वैष्णवः । कौलं गोपायेद्वेवि नारिकेलफलाम्बुवत् ॥ XI. 83. One ms. reads अन्तःकौला बहिःशैवाः सभायां वैष्णवा मताः and this last is often quoted. The कौलावलीनिर्णय (X. 84–95) reads अन्तःशाक्ता बहिःशैवाः...मताः । नानावेशधराः कौला विचरन्ति महीतले ॥. The sect mark of both Śaivas and Śāktas is tripuṇḍra (three parallel lines of holy ashes on the forehead from one eye to the other, drawn with three fingers other than the thumb and small finger). Vide बृहज्जाबालोपनिषद् IV. 10–11, देवीभागवत XI. 15. 17–23.]]

[^1745]: Vide a paper of D. C. Bhattacharya in J. G. J. R. I. [[vol, VI|vol. VI]] pp. [[241-247|241–247]] on Vidyāpati's work on Tantra. The first verse of पुरुषपरीक्षा (Darbhanga ed. of 1888) is [[' ब्रह्मापि यां नौति सुतः सराणां ( सुराणां ?) यामर्चितोप्यर्थयतीन्दुमौलीः । यां ध्यायति ध्यानगतोपि विष्णुस्तामादिशक्ति शिरसा प्रणौमि ॥'|'ब्रह्मापि यां नौति सुतः सराणां (सुराणां ?) यामर्चितोप्यर्थयतीन्दुमौलीः । यां ध्यायति ध्यानगतोपि विष्णुस्तामादिशक्तिं शिरसा प्रणौमि ॥']]

[[P1077]]king Ballālasena who discarded [[Devipurāṇa|Devīpurāṇa]] as an authority in his encyclopaedic work on gifts called [[Dānasāgara.1746|Dānasāgara.]][^1746]

It is possible that the founders of the Tāntrik or [[Sākta|Śākta]] cult of the five makāras threw defiance at the terrible aspect of God or the Highest Spirit, that rules the destinies of men and things and that made men sometimes suffer terribly even if they led lives of virtue, to do the worst for their disregard of conventional morality and social practices and hoped by their Yoga exercises to attain to high psychical powers and [[bliss. 1747|bliss.]][^1747] There is another possible motive also. Masses of common people were being drawn towards Buddhism. The founders of Hindu Tāntrik cults wanted to retain them within the Hindu fold. As common people drank wine and ate meat, they were told that they would attain higher spiritual levels even while indulging in meat and drink provided they followed Tāntrik gurus and practices. The idea was that [[Sakti|Śakti]] was all and was for all; bhoga (enjoyment) need not be given up, as man is part of Devi or Śiva. Bhoga should be sublimated, that is all that is required in [[Kaula-śāstra.|Kaulaśāstra.]] The [[Tāntrikas 1748|Tāntrikas]][^1748] substitute a yoga of enjoyment (bhoga) for the yoga of abstinence and asceticism. When indulging in the left-hand path practices the sādhaka is supposed or held to be destroying the egoistic elements of the soul.

The Mahānirvāṇatantra and a few others endeavour to stem the tide of sexual immorality and promiscuity. For example, Rāmeśvara, the commentator of Paraśurāmakalpasūtra, says that one that has not conquered his senses has no adhikāra for Kaulamārga (p. 153). This is in direct conflict with what even the Mahānirvāṇatantra says that all men from brāhmaṇas to the untouchables have adhikāra for Kula practices. Modern apologists for Tāntrism emphasize that the instructions embodied in the Guhyasamāja, the directions for the mode of life to be led by followers of Vajrayāna, apply only to the Yogis who have attained some degree of yogic perfection. But the obvious

[^1746]: नानावेशधराः कौलाः कुलाचारेषु निश्चलाः । सेवन्ते तां कुलाचारैर्नहि तान् बाधते कलिः ॥ महानिर्वाणतन्त्र IV. 63.

[^1747]: Vide Dr. B. Bhattacharya's Intro. to Guhyasamāja p. XXII for the above sentiments.

[^1748]: यत्रास्ति भोगो न च तत्र मोक्षो यत्रास्ति मोक्षो न च तत्र भोगः। [[श्रीसुन्दरीसेवनतत्पराणां|श्रीसुन्दरीसेवन-तत्पराणां]] भोगश्च मोक्षश्च करस्थ एव ॥ quoted in हंसविलास (from कौलरहस्य) p. 104; विप्राद्यन्त्यजपर्यन्ता द्विपदा येऽत्र भूतले । ते सर्वेऽस्मिन्कुलाचारे भवेयुरधिकारिणः ॥ महानिर्वाण 14. 184.

[[P1078]]reply is 'who is to decide that a particular person had attained the minimum spiritual level except the man himself and, [[pupposing|supposing]] that all the directions were meant for Yogis, why was it necessary to say in a blatant manner and language that a Yogi practising [[Vajrayana|Vajrayāna]] may engage in what common and less sophisticated people deem to be incest? This is not the place to reply to all apologists for ancient and medieval Tāntrik works. But a few must be dealt with because they are liable to create misunderstandings, if allowed to go uncriticized. In his Preface to 'Principles of Tantra' Part 2, Sir John Woodroffe [[statos|states]] (p. IX.) that the use of flesh, fish and wine was common in the Vedic age and that the Mahābhārata and some Purāṇas like the Kālikā, [[Mārkandeya|Mārkaṇḍeya]] and Kūrma refer to consumption of wine, meat and fish. This looks like special pleading and is misleading. The question is: was [[sirū|surā]] offered to the gods in every day or periodic sacrifices as an offering in the [[Roveda|Ṛgveda]] or any other Veda. In the Vedic age wine may have been known and even drunk, but what is material to recognize is that there is a difference made between Soma and surā. [[Vida Sat. 176 Br.|Vide Śat.]][^1749] Br. "Soma is truth, prosperity, light and surā is untruth, [[nigary|misery]] and darkness" (V. 1. 5. 28). While Soma is mentioned hundreds of times in the Ṛgveda, has the 9th [[mandala|maṇḍala]] of the Ṛgveda specially reserved for its praises and was offered to gods, the word [[surū|surā]] occurs only six times in the Ṛgveda and it is nowhere expressly stated that it was offered to any god as [[&|a]] religious offering; on the contrary, in a hymn to Varuṇa, [[burā|surā]] is put forward as leading to the commission of sin just as anger and gambling do (Ṛg. VII. 86. 6 'na sa [[svo.|svo]] [[dakso|dakṣo]] [[Varuna|Varuṇa]] [[dhrutih|dhrutiḥ]] sā surā manyur-vibhidako [[acittih).|acittiḥ').]] In [[bis|his]] zeal for the vindication of Tāntrism Arthur Avalon is injudicious enough to misinterpret simple words. In the Intro. to 'Principles of Tantra' p. VII [[be|he]] quotes Ṛg. I. 166. 7 "arcantyarkam madirasya [[pitaye"|pītaye"]] and translates "worshipping the Sun before drinking [[madira (wine)."|madirā (wine)."]] The word here is *madira* (and not 'madirā'), it is an adjective and means "exhilarating". The word “Madirā" never occurs in the Ṛg. while "madira" as an adjective, occurs at least sixteen times and qualifies Soma, Indu, [[Amsu,|Aṁśu,]] Rasa or Madhu, generally expressed (and rarely implied). There is no word for "before." That quarter means "they (the worshippers or Maruts) worship (Indra) who is worthy of praise (and a friend of the Maruts) in order that he may come for

[^1749]: [[H 

r vatia: HATSTE TOT A: Timu V, 1, 5, 28.|सत्यं वै श्रीर्ज्योतिः सोमोऽनृतं पाप्मा तमः सुरा । शतपथ V. 1. 5. 28.]]

[[P1079]]drinking the exhilarating [[(Soma).|(Soma)'.]] The word madirā (for [[wine )|wine)]] hardly ever occurs in any [[genuing|genuine]] work of Vedic times. Its earliest [[oocurrence|occurrence]] is probably in the [[Mahābbārate.|Mahābhārata.]] Reference is also made by modern apologists to the offering of surā in the [[Sautrāmaṇl iṣti|Sautrāmaṇī iṣṭi]] in honour of [[Indre|Indra.]] But the circumstances are peculiar. Sautrāmaṇī is only one out of numerous sacrifices and the occasions for performing it were rare [[i.e.|i. e.]] it was performed at the end of [[Rājagūya|Rājasūya]] and also at the end of Agnicayana and when a priest who had drunk too much of soma vomited. The most important fact, however, is that the remnants of surā offered in Sautrāmaṇī were not drunk by the priests engaged in it but a brāhmaṇa [[bad|had]] to be hired for drinking the remnants and if no brāhmaṇa could be secured then the remnants were to be poured over an ant-hill (vide H. of Dh. vol. II, p. 1226 for details). The [[Kathakagamhitā 1750|Kāṭhakasaṁhitā]][^1750] contains an interesting passage "Therefore an elderly person and a youngster, the daughter-in-law and the father-in-law, drink liquor and remain babbling together; thoughtlessness is sin; therefore a brāhmaṇa does not drink surā with the thought that 'otherwise (if I drink it) I may be tainted by sin'; therefore this is for [[ksatriya;|kṣatriya;]] one should say to a brāhmaṇa that surā, if drunk by [[&|a]] [[ksatriya,|kṣatriya,]] does not harm the latter." These passages show that not only the priests did not drink surā even in [[Sautrāmapi|Sautrāmaṇī]] but that it had become difficult to hire [[& brāhmapa|a brāhmaṇa]] for drinking it by the time of the [[Kathaka-gamhitā.|Kāṭhaka-saṁhitā.]] The passage of the Vājasaneya Saṁhitā 19. 5 relied on at p. VII (of Intro [[to|to 'Principles]] of Tantra' part II) also refers to [[Saytrāmaṇi|Sautrāmaṇī]] and to no other [[gagri fice.|sacrifice.]] The mantra is [["Brahma kṣatram pavate toja, indriyam surayā - somaḥ suta asuto madāya"|'Brahmā kṣatraṁ pavate teja indriyaṁ surayā somaḥ suta āsuto madāya']] and means that: "Soma when mixed with surā becomes a strong drink and leads to [[intoxication."|intoxication.']] The Chāndogya V. 10. [[9' (q.|9 (q.]] above p. 1578 n. [[943')|943)]] counts the drinker of surā among the five grave sinners. [[There fore,|Therefore,]] there is hardly any analogy between the offering of wine in Sautrāmaṇī and the teaching about offering [[wine. to Doyl|wine to Devī]] as provided in the Tantras. Similarly, the reference to the [[Atharyaveda|Atharvaveda]] as containing magical [[rites. does|rites does]] not help at all. Society had advanced far beyond [[that-stage|that stage]] and Manu XI. 63 looked upon abhicāra (i. [[o.|e.]] performance of a [[magio|magic]] rite like Śyenayāga for killing a person) and [[mūlakārmda|mūlakarma]] (i. e. bringing

[^1750]: [[Alleretna safraison 4 er get stat & Stora HAI.. पाप्मा मालप्यं तस्माद मामणः पुरा न पिवति पाप्मना नेससज्या इति तदेतत् क्षधियाचं migroi raritat ett ofar RARIO RAT XII. 12.|तस्माज्ज्येष्ठश्च कनीयांश्च स्नुषा श्वशुरश्च सुरां पीत्वा सह लालपत आसते। पाप्मा वै मालक्ष्यं तस्माद् ब्राह्मणः सुरां न पिबति पाप्मना नेत्संसृज्या इति तदेतत् क्षत्रियाय ब्राह्मणं ब्रूयान्नैनं सुरा पीता हिनस्ति । काठकसंहिता XII. 12.]]

[[P1080]]under one's control a person or a woman by herbs and mantras) as a sin, though a [[legger|lesser]] one (an upapātaka). The reference to the Mahābhārata [[(Udyogaparya,| (Udyogaparva,]] 59. 5 ff) is again misleading. Wine was drunk by people in the Mahābhārata times, but not as part of a religious ritual as in the Tantras. Vide pp. 964–966 of H. of Dh. vol. III for a note on 'intoxicating drinks.' The reference to Mārkaṇḍeya and other purāṇas is of no avail as evidence, since at least the present author holds that those parts of the Purāṇas were written and interpolated after Tāntrik rites had taken a firm hold on some sections of the Hindu society. The reference to sexual intercourse in the Mahāvrata[^1751] is most misleading. In the Tantras like the Kulārṇava and the [[Guhya. samāja|Guhyasamāja]] it is the sādhaka himself who is to practise maithuna as one of the ways of securing marvellous powers and higher spiritual attainments. But in the Mahāvrata the sexual intercourse is by strangers to the sacrifice (and not by the yajamāna nor by any of the [[priests ),|priests),]] is a purely symbolical act and [[beans 20|bears no]] analogy to a sādhaka himself engaging in maithuna as a religious rite to placate the Devī. Even [[&|a]] late reformist work like the [[Mahānispāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] (VIII. 174–175) expressly says that those of the five tattvas which a sādhaka can secure, such as flesh and others, should have recited over them the mantra 'ām, hrīm, krom, [[svābā'|svāhā']] a hundred times, that he should reflect that every thing is [[&|a]] product of *brahma*, should close his eyes, should offer those to Kālī and should himself drink and eat them. As the Tāntrik prescriptions of the makāras for attainment of [[mira culous|miraculous]] powers and of liberation had shocked people and [[bad|had]] brought the tantras into great disrepute, later Hindu Tāntrik works like the Śaktisaṅgamatantra (between 1555–1607 A. D.) began to offer symbolic interpretations. They[^1752] say that the

[^1751]: Vide H. of [[Db,|Dh.]] Vol. II pp 1243–1245 for the [[Mabāvrata,|Mahāvrata,]] which is the last day but one in a sattra.

I... turgaretata quergeracoat Wo, atreve 32.25-27, 32. Vide . Sakti and Sākta ' pp. 339-340 where madga, māsa, matsya and mal thona are interpreted esoterically for a divyabhāva' following Yoginitantra (cbap. VI) and from Āganasāra. Ono verse from the former may be quoted 

(Continued on next page)|गुडार्द्रकरसो देवि मुद्रा तु प्रथमा मता। पिण्याकं लवणं देवि द्वितीया परिकीर्तिता। लशुनं तित्तिडी चैव तृतीया परिकीर्तिता । गोधूममाषसम्भूता सुन्दरी च चतुर्थिका । शक्त्यालापः पञ्चमी स्यात्पञ्च मुद्राः प्रकीर्तिताः ॥ शक्तिसङ्गम, ताराखण्ड, 32, 13–15; vide महानिर्वाणतन्त्र VI. 9–10 where products of grains of rice, barley or wheat with ghee or fried grain are called मुद्रा. न मद्यं माधवीमद्यं मद्यं शक्तिरसोद्भवम् । सुषुम्ना शङ्खिनी मुद्रा उन्मन्यमृतमं रसः ॥ सामरस्यामतोल्लासं मैथुनं च सदाशिवम् । महाकुण्डलिनी शक्तिस्तद्योगार्थं महेश्वरि ।...संयोगोऽमृतयोगेन कुण्डल्युत्थानकारणात् । शक्तिसङ्गम०, ताराखण्ड 32. 25–27, 32. Vide 'Śakti and Śākta' pp. 339–340 where madya, māṁsa, matsya and maithuna are interpreted esoterically for a 'divyabhāva' following Yoginītantra (chap. VI) and from Āgamasāra. One verse from the former may be quoted: 'सहस्रारोपरि बिन्दौ कुण्डल्या मेलनं शिवे । मैथुनं परमं द्रव्यं यतीनां परिकीर्तितम् ॥'. Substitutional तत्त्वs are also variously mentioned for पशु, who is the lowest kind of the worshippers of Śakti. The कौलावलीनिर्णय V. 113–123 suggests various substitutes viz. a ब्राह्मण may employ honey in a copper vessel or cow's milk or the water of cocoanut fruit in a bell-metal vessel; that, in the absence of flesh, garlic and ginger may be used for flesh, milk of buffalo or sheep for fish, roasted fruits and roots for maithuna. It will be, however, noticed in the above and also later on that the explanations do not all completely agree and therefore raise doubts about their veracity.]]

[[P1081]]words '[[medya,|madya,]] [[mudrā, maithuna' &o.|mudrā, maithuna' &c.]] are not used in [[this|the]] ordinary popular sense but in a special [[esoterio|esoteric]] [[sense. .. For|sense. For]] example, mudrā has several [[sensos,|senses,]] viz. a mixture of jaggery and ginger, or of salt and oil-cake or garlic and tamarind fruit, or wheat and māṣa beans, that madya (wine) is not what is prepared from [[mādbavi (|mādhavī (]]spring [[flower )|flower)]] but it springs from the joyful experience (or *rasa*) of [[Sakti,|Śakti,]] when effort is made to awaken the [[Kundalini.|Kuṇḍalinī.]] It may be admitted that some Tāntrik works and writers divide men into three classes, paśu (the bestial ones), [[vira|vīra]] (those that have made great progress on the path of spiritual discipline) and daiva (those who are godlike). As regards these three, the five makāras are assigned by some [[apologistio|apologistic]] writers different [[meaninge.|meanings.]] D. N. [[Boge|Bose]] in his work *Tantras, their philosophy and occult secrets' [[boldly' asserts|boldly asserts]] (p. 110) that the real significance of the five makāras has been deliberately perverted by vicious people and explains [[(p: 111)|(p. 111)]] that *madya* is the nectarine stream issuing from the cavity of the brain where the soul resides, matsya means suppression of vital airs, māṁsa means [[' yow|'vow]] of silence,' 'maithuna' means 'meditation on the acts of creation and [[destruction. ..:|destruction.']]

The Tāntrikas clothe their practices in bombastic and [[high. sounding|high-sounding]] words. The five makāras are called pañca-tattvas, kuladravyas or kulatattvas. Maithuna is generally referred to [[28 parcamatattva,|as pañcamatattva,]] the woman with whom sexual intercourse [[iṣ|is]] to be had or who was at least to be associated with a male in Tantra worship is called [[Sakti|Śakti]] (vide [[Kulārnava VII 39-43|Kulārṇava VII 39–43]] and [[Mahānirvana|Mahānirvāṇa]] VI. [[18-20)|18–20)]] or [[praksti|prakṛti]] or latā and this special ritual is called 'Latāsādhana' (Mahānirvāṇa I. 52). Wine is called [[tirthavāri|tīrthavāri]] (holy water) or kāraṇa (VIII. 168 and VI. [[17.)|17).]] The [[Mahānirvana-tantra,|Mahānirvāṇa-tantra,]] though a reformist work and in certain [[oases|cases]] asking the king to punish drunkards (XI. 113–121),

[[P1082]]waxes eloquent over wine and praises it as [[Tara|Tārā]] in the [[form 1750 of a|form]][^1753] of a fluid substance, as the saviour of individual souls, as the mother (or producer) of bhoga (enjoyment) and mokṣa ([[libera tion)|liberation)]] and as the remover of misfortunes and diseases and after some further praise of wine winds up by saying that those men who drink wine according to the rules and with a well-controlled mind are gods on earth (XI. 108). For the worship of [[Sakti|Śakti]] the five tattvas are absolutely necessary ([[Mahānirvana-tantra|Mahānirvāṇa-tantra]] V. [[21-24|21–24]] quoted in note 1695 above and[^1754] Kulārṇava V. 69 and 76). Some tantras say that the meaning of tattva differs according as the person concerned is [[Tamasika|Tāmasika]] (a paśu kind of worshipper), Rājasika (a [[vira|vīra]] as he is called), sāttvika (a divya, a person who is godlike). Madya means according to various Tāntrik Texts real wine as well as a substitute like cocoanut water or any other liquid; it also means that intoxicating [[know ledge|knowledge]] that comes of Yoga practices, whereby the worshipper becomes senseless as regards the external world. [[Maisa|Māṁsa]] is the [[Bot|act]] whereby the sādhaka surrenders himself and his action to Lord [[Śiva|Śiva.]] *Matsya* (fish, of which the first part 'mat' means [['mino')|'mine')]] is that psychical state by which the worshipper [[sympa thizes|sympathizes]] with the pleasure and pain of all beings. Maithuna is the union of the [[Sakti Kundalini|Śakti Kuṇḍalinī]] (the woman inside a man's body) in the [[Mūlādbāra-cakra|Mūlādhāra-cakra]] with the supreme Śiva in the [[Sahasraracakra|Sahasrāracakra]] in the highest centre of the brain and is the stream of the sweet juice that drops from the [[Sabasrāra.|Sahasrāra.]] [[Preparar tion|Preparation]] of hemp (called vijayā or [['bhang')|'bhāṅg')]] is madya for some. The [[Mahāniryāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] says (VIII. 170 and 173) that 'madhura-traya' may be substituted for wine and for 'Maithuna' meditation on the [[foot|feet]] of the [[image of ) Devi|image of Devī]] and japa of the desired mantra may be substituted. The [[Kaulavallnirṇaya|Kaulāvalīnirṇaya]] (III. 111) boldly states that if a man, after partaking of [[vijaya (bhāng) ongages|vijayā (bhāṅg) engages]] in meditation, he sees directly before him the form of the goddess as described in the Dhyānamantra. Some Tantras like the

[^1754]: [[कुलदम्पविना कुर्याजपयज्ञतपोवतम् । निन्फलं तवेददेवि भल्मनीव पचास मन्त्रपूर्व कुलमान्य गुरुदेषापितं पिये। पिवन्ति जनास्तेषां स्वन्यपान विद्यते। कुलार्णव. 69 and 76. The words FT ... faga mean that he is not born again. The gerere (V.79–80) ordaldı '80 siuni: forat na sa Hai Tere सहपानन्दो मोकाउन्यते। भानन्द बाणो सर्प नचदेहे ज्यवस्थितम् । तस्माभिम्पर्क मयं योमिमिस्तेन पीपते.|कुलद्रव्यैर्विना कुर्याज्जपयज्ञतपोव्रतम् । निष्फलं तद्भवेद्देवि भस्मनीव यथा हुतम् ॥ मन्त्रपूतं कुलद्रव्यं गुरुदेवार्पितं प्रिये । ये पिबन्ति जनास्तेषां स्तन्यपानं न विद्यते ॥ कुलार्णव V. 69 and 76. [[P1083]][[Kaulajñananirṇaya|Kaulajñānanirṇaya]] and [[Bhaskarariya|Bhāskararāya]][^1755] in his commentary on [[Lalitigahasranama|Lalitāsahasranāma]] say that when [[Kundalini in|Kuṇḍalinī is]] roused by [[&|a]] yogin and it reaches the [[thousand-patalled|thousand-petalled]] cakra [[(Sahasrara )|(Sahasrāra)]] from [[whioh|which]] (that has on its pericarp the moon) drops [[neotar|nectar]] which is figuratively spoken of [[aa|as]] *madya* (vide notes 1716, 1752). The [[Kularnava|Kulārṇava]] at first asserts (I. [[105-107)|105–107)]] 'Mukti does not result from the study of the Veda nor by the study of śāstras, it results from (correct) knowledge alone, that the āśramas are not a means of [[moksa|mokṣa]] nor are the [[darsanas|darśanas]] the means, nor all the śāstras, that it is knowledge alone that is the cause, that it is the knowledge imparted by the teaching of the guru that confers *mukti*, all (other) vidyās are mockery.' Then in the Vedāntic strain it affirms (I. [[111-112 )|111–112)]] “two words (respectively) lead to bondage or liberation viz. (this is) 'mine' or 'nothing is [[mine '.|mine'.]] A person falls in bondage by the thought 'this is mine' and becomes liberated by realising 'nothing is mine' and that that is proper action that does not lead to bondage and that is real lore that leads to [[liberation."|liberation.']] After these high thoughts the same Tantra (II. [[22-23|22–23]] and 29) proceeds to Kaula doctrine. 'If a man is a yogin he does not (ordinarily) enjoy life, while one who enjoys life does not know yoga; but the Kaula doctrine combines both *yoga* and *bhoga* and is superior to all (other doctrines); in the Kaula doctrine bhoga turns into yoga directly, what is sin (acc. to ordinary people) becomes meritorious, [[samsāra|saṁsāra]] turns into mokṣa. Kaula knowledge enlightens him whose mind is purified by the mantras of Śaiva worship, Durgā worship [[&c. "|&c.']]

The [[Kulārnava|Kulārṇava]] appears to be in two minds to ordinary men like the present writer. While in one breath as shown in note 1718 it frankly recommends the drinking of wine and eating of flesh to the followers of Kaula doctrine, it also endeavours to give an esoteric meaning to the makāras as follows (V. [[107-112):|107–112):]] Frequently reaching the *brahmarandhra* from the *mūlādhāra*, there arises the bliss of the union of the [[Kundalini-Sakti|Kuṇḍalinī-Śakti]] with the moon in the form of Intelligence (*cit*, [[Śiva );|Śiva);]] the person who is intent on tasting the nectar oozing from the lotus in the

[^1755]: [[सा (कुण्डलिनी) हि मूलाधाराख्येच सार्वत्रिवलयाकारेण सुहासती पोगिभिर त्याप्य पदचकाणि पिण्याविबन्धींश्च भेदयन्ती सहसार नीता सती बकर्णिकारूपचन्म HOUSE TRAI p. 52 of TAKTY'S com. Er Hent on THE FREITA ON verses 87-91 referent ... FUTTATHIRT Tathroarranquit. ... HETETTE guapisetit Pohonartu (Nir, ed, of 1935). Compare कौलज्ञाननिर्णय VI. 18–19 p. 16.]]

[[P1084]][[orown|crown]] of the head is said to drink *sudhā* ([[neotar,|nectar,]] wine); other people drink [[nere|mere]] wine. When the adept[^1756] in Yoga cutting the beast (ego) doing good or evil actions with the sword of knowledge (of reality) makes his mind merge in the Highest, he is said to be eater of pala (the Highest, flesh). The (Yogin) who restraining the several senses by his mind concentrates them on the Self, becomes 'matsyāśī',[^1757] others are only killers of living beings. The [[Sakti|Śakti]] (the woman associated with a [[sādhaka )|sādhaka)]] of a beastly man is unenlightened but that of the Kaulika is enlightened; he who honours (or resorts to) such a [[Sakti|Śakti]] is really a worshipper of [[Sakti.|Śakti.]] When a man is filled through and through with the bliss due to the union of the Highest [[Sakti|Śakti]] with the Self ([[Śiva ),|Śiva),]] that is called Maithuna [[(copulation );|(copulation);]] all other men are merely licentious persons.

The apologists for unpopular Tāntrik practices offer explanations of the five 'makāras' more or less on the lines of the [[Kulār-anava.|Kulārṇava.]] For example, in his Introduction to the 'Principles of Tantra' (part II) Arthur Avalon (Sir John Woodroffe) gives (pp. [[CVII-CVIII )|CVII–CVIII)]] an esoteric explanation of the word 'drinking' used in such a verse as that in the Pārānandasūtra, 'Having drunk again and again, having fallen upon the earth, then having got up and again drunk (wine) there is no [[rebirth.' 1758|rebirth.']][^1758]

[^1756]: [[आमूलाधारमाबारन्धं गत्वा पुनः पुनः । चिश्चन्द्रकुण्डलीशकिसामरस्य सुखोदयः। ग्योमपङ्कजनिस्यन्दसुधापानरतो नरः। सुधापानमिदं प्रोक्तमितरे मद्यपापिनः । पुण्यापुण्यपशुं हत्वा ज्ञानखड्नेन योगवित् । परे लयं नयेचित्तं पलाशी स निगद्यते । मनसा चेन्द्रियगणं संयम्यात्मनि योजयेत् । मत्स्याशी स भवेद्देवि शेषाः स्युः प्राणिहिंसकाः । अप्रबुद्धा पशोः शक्तिः प्रबुद्धा कौलिकस्य च । शक्ति तां सेपयेद्यस्तु स भवेच्छक्तिसेवकः । पराशक्त्यात्म मिथुनसंयोगानन्दनिर्भरः। य आस्ते मैथुनं तत् स्यादपरे स्त्रीनिषेवकाः ॥ कुलार्णव V. 107-112.|आमूलाधारमाब्रह्मरन्ध्रं गत्वा पुनः पुनः । चिच्चन्द्रकुण्डलीशक्तिसामरस्यसुखोद्यः ॥ व्योमपङ्कजनिर्यत्सुधापानरतो नरः । सुधापानमिदं प्रोक्तमितरे मद्यपायिनः ॥ पुण्यापुण्यपशुं हत्वा ज्ञानखड्गेन योगवित् । परे लयं नयेच्चित्तं पलाशी स निगद्यते ॥ मनसा चेन्द्रियगणं संयम्यात्मनि योजयेत् । मत्स्याशी स भवेद्देवि शेषाः स्युः प्राणिहिंसकाः ॥ अप्रबुद्धा पशोः शक्तिः प्रबुद्धा कौलिकस्य च । शक्तिं तां सेवयेद्यस्तु स भवेच्छक्तिसेवकः ॥ पराशक्त्यात्ममिथुनसंयोगानन्दनिर्भरः । य आस्ते मैथुनं तत् स्यादपरे स्त्रीनिषेवकाः ॥ कुलार्णव V. 107–112.]] The 4th tattva is Mudrā, but the latter word is often applied to the [[Sakti|Śakti]] associated with a sādhaka.

[^1757]: [['Palasi'|'Palāśī']] means 'eater or enjoyer of Pala.' Pala means [['flesh'.|'flesh',]] and pala stands for Para (Highest) [['ai'|'aś']] r and l are often interchangeable in Sanskrit and the root [['aś|'aś']] may mean 'to reach' as well as 'to [[cat.|eat.]] [[Matsyasi|Matsyāśī]] literally means 'eater of fish,' but in the esoteric interpretation "matsya" stands for 'manas' (mind) plus 'sya' representing [['samyama'|'saṁyama']] (restraint).

(Continued from last page) quotes from ... gaia , and takes it to a literal sense by citing ā passag|जीवन्मुक्तः पिवेद्देवमन्यथा पतितो भवेदिति । पुनः पीत्वा पुनः पीत्वा पतित्वा धरणीतले । उत्थाय च पुनः पीत्वा पुनर्जन्म न विद्यते ॥ पारानन्दसूत्र p. 17 sūtras 81–82; the Intro. to 'Principles of Tantra' (part II) p. CVIII. It reads the first half of the verse as पीत्वा पीत्वा पुनः पीत्वा पीत्वा पतति भूतले. But there is hardly any difference in the literal meaning. Vide कुलार्णवतन्त्र VII. 100 which reads पीत्वा पीत्वा...यावत्पतति भूतले.' रामेश्वर, commentator of परशुराम-कल्पसूत्र, quotes पीत्वा...पतति भूतले, and takes it in a literal sense by citing a passage आगलान्तं पिबेद् द्रव्यं (on V. 22 p. 213) from कुलार्णव VII. 99. कौलावलीतन्त्र (VIII. 164–165) has this verse but reads पतित्वा च महीतले. Verses 213–214 refer to the fifth makāra in cakrapūjā and verses 223–225 justify promiscuous intercourse in Śrīcakra and verse 226 says 'it is the sinful vāsanā that is reprehensible, but to the pure-hearted everything is pure (omnia sancta sanctis)'. The कालिविलासतन्त्र (ed. by A. Avalon, 1917 Tāntrik Texts, vol. VI.) has the verse पीत्वा पीत्वा...पुनः पीत्वा पञ्चमं सत्यसम्मतम् । (VI. 25).]]

[[P1085]]He explains 'Being thus awakened [[Kundalinl|Kuṇḍalinī]] enters the great road to liberation (mukti), that is, the [[Sushumna|Sushumnā]] nerve, and penetrating the centres one by one, ascends to the [[Sahasrara|Sahasrāra]] and there coming in blissful communion with the Lord of Lords, again descends down through the same passage to the Mūlādhāra Chakra. Nectar is said to flow from such communion. The sādhaka drinks it and becomes supremely happy. This is the wine called Kulāmṛta, which a sādhaka of the spiritual plane drinks......In reference to a sādhaka of the spiritual ([[ādhyātmika )|ādhyātmika)]] class the Tantra says [[‘Pitvā pitvā...vidyate’.|'Pītvā pītvā...vidyate'.]] During the first stage of [[Şat-cakra-sādhanā|Ṣaṭ-cakra-sādhanā]] the sādhaka cannot suppress his breath for a sufficiently long time at a stretch to enable him to practise concentration and meditation in each centre of Power. He cannot therefore detain [[Kundalini|Kuṇḍalinī]] within the Sushumnā longer than his power of Kumbhaka permits. He must consequently come down upon the earth i. e. the Mūlādhāra, which is the centre of the element earth, after having drunk of the heavenly ambrosia. The sādhaka must practise this again and again and by constant practice, the cause of rebirth i. e. vāsanā (desire) is [[removed."|removed.']] This explanation sounds very profound and highly psychical, but it is not at all convincing. Similarly, the present author wonders how many writers on Tantras and how many Tāntrikas understood the theory of sublimation put forward in 'Tantras as a way of realization' (Cultural Heritage of India, Vol. IV. pp. [[233–235 )|233–235)]] by way of explaining the insistence on five makāras. The first question is: why was it necessary to employ vulgar language to describe a state of profound bliss? Supposing the explanation of madya offered by Woodroffe is accepted, what is the explanation about offering flesh and fish? One cannot easily get out of [['matsyāśi'|'matsyāśī']] and [['māmsāśi'|'māṁsāśī']] the esoteric sense required by the apologists. The [[Kulārnava,|Kulārṇava,]] the Pārānanda-sūtra and several

[[P1086]]other works almost always employ the words madya, [[mānga|māṁsa]] and fish in the ordinary [[sen86.|sense.]] They rarely, if at all, drop the hint that words like matsya and māṁsa are being used in an esoteric sense and proceed to deal with realities of madya and flesh. The Kulārṇava quotes (II. 126) the verse of Manu IX. 93 (surā vai malam-annānām &c.), provides for the preparation of the three kinds of surā (V. [[15–21 ),|15–21),]] and says (V. 30) that surā is the 12th kind among intoxicants and that there are eleven other kinds of intoxicants made from jack-fruit, grapes, dates, sugar-cane &c. (V. 29). In XI. 22–35 the Kulārṇava dilates upon the etiquette to be observed in drinking wine as part of Kaula practice. It proceeds (V. 44) to divide flesh into three classes viz. of animals that fly in the sky (birds), of [[acquatic|aquatic]] animals and of those that move on the earth. The[^1759] Svacchandatantra (a work of great authority in Kashmir [[Saivism )|Śaivism)]] prescribes that various kinds of fish and meat and other food that can be licked and drunk should be poured in front of Śiva (image) and one should not stint for money. Quotations from Pārānanda-sūtra (notes 1698, [[1703-4 )|1703–4)]] make it perfectly clear that the words madya, [[māmsa,|māṁsa,]] and maithuna are employed therein in the ordinary sense. Pārānandasūtra (on pp. [[80-81,|80–81,]] paragraphs [[69-70|69–70]] and pp. [[82-83|82–83]] para. [[76-80 )|76–80)]] describes such obscene details of the sādhaka's sexual intercourse that it is not possible to set them down here. The ordinary worship of [[Devi|Devī]] was elaborate enough, containing as it did 16 upacāras (vide p. 164 note 420 above). Where was the necessity of bringing in wine, meat and maithuna as an absolutely necessary part of the worship of [[Devi ?|Devī?]] The Kulārṇava and other tantras praise the Vedas, employ vedic mantras and [[quote 1760|quote]][^1760] Upaniṣad and Gītā passages. For example, Mahānirvāṇa V. 197 ff employs Vedic mantras (as noted on p. 1058) for sanctifying the five makāras, the three mantras 'Āpo hi [[stha'|ṣṭhā']] Ṛg. X. 9. 1–3 in Nāmakaraṇa (ibid. IX. [[150-152 ),|150–152),]] 'Tac-cakṣur' Ṛg. VII. 66. 16 in Niṣkramaṇa (ibid. IX. [[163 ),|163),]] 'Viṣṇuryonim' Ṛg. X. 184. 1 in Garbhādhāna (ibid. IX. [[96 ),|96),]] 'Ayam te yonir' Ṛg. III. 29. 10 in the worship of Agni (ibid. IX. [[21 )|21)]] and the verse 'Kālī Karālī' (Muṇḍakopaniṣad I. 2. 4)

[^1759]: [[मत्स्यमांसाम्यनेकानि लेह्यपेयानि पानि अग्रमापूरयेच्छाम्भोक्तिशान. frefera: Fuqar 11. (22) 135 (Kashmir S. series with the com, yora of क्षेमराज), मथं मांस तथा मत्स्यानन्यानि चपरानने। साचारांश निराचारविनिोन yuntai ibid V, 48,|मत्स्यमांसाण्यनेकानि लेह्यपेयानि यानि च । अग्रेणापूरयेच्छम्भोर्वित्तशाठ्यविवर्जितः ॥ स्वच्छन्दतन्त्र II. (पटल) 135 (Kashmir S. series with the com. उद्योत of क्षेमराज); मद्यं मांसं तथा मत्स्यान्मद्यानि च वरानने । साचारांश्च निराचारांस्त्रिलिङ्गिनो न जुगुप्सयेत् । ibid V. 48.]]

[^1760]: [[Femeia XII. 33 cites the verse per ty &c. (which is made VI. 23): vide anfare for II. 44-45 for adopting d. 79. framerat que 

fittara ed: 1 ... gegrerafa i|कुलार्णव XII. 33 cites the verse यस्य देवे &c. (which is श्वेताश्वतरोप. VI. 23); vide महानिर्वाण II. 44–45 for adopting तै. उप. 'भीषास्माद्वातः पवते भीषोदेति सूर्यः । ...मृत्युर्धावति पञ्चमः ।']]

[[P1087]]in the worship of the seven tongues of Agni. The [[Mabānirvāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] (VIII. [[214-215 ) proscribes Gita|214–215) prescribes Gītā]] IV. 24 (brahmārpaṇam brahma havir) as the mantra to be recited by the leader of the Tattvacakra in offering the five tattvas and in sanctifying them. In spite of all this lip service to the Veda the tantras ignore the great moral dangers involved in their persistent teaching of five makāras and also pay no heed to the impressive advice of the Gītā (III. 21) that whatever a great or honoured man does or holds as authoritative is followed by all common people. There are several late medieval works about the Kaula cult that speak of drinking wine, eating flesh and maithuna in the vulgar sense as means of [[Devi|Devī]] worship and yet assert that by doing so mukti (liberation) would be secured. Two verses from a ms. of a tāntrik work called Kaularahasya (containing one hundred verses) will bring home to the reader how common people understood the cult of the [[makāras.1761|makāras.]][^1761]

Prof. Heinrich Zimmer in the 'Art of Indian Asia' (vol. I pp. 129–130) observes 'such living forms are suggested to the Indian artist by a dynamic philosophy that is intrinsic to his religious and philosophical tradition, for the worship of the [[lifeforce|life-force]] pouring into the universe and maintaining it, manifesting itself no less in the gross matter of daily experience than in the divine beings of religious vision constitutes the very foundation of Indian religious life. According to this doctrine which was particularly influential in the great periods of Indian art, release from the bondage of our normal human imperfection can be gained not only through the [[worldnegating|world-negating]] methods of asceticism (yoga) but equally through a perfect realization of love and its sexual enjoyment (bhoga). According to this view which has been eloquently expressed in the so-called Tāntric symbols and rituals of both the Hindu and Buddhist traditions, there is intrinsically no antagonism between yoga and bhoga. The role played by the guru, the spiritual guide and teacher in the stern masculine disciplines of yoga, is

[^1761]: [[निधाय धारा पदने सुधायाः श्रीचक्रमम्पर्य कुलकमेण। आखाय मय पिशित मुगाक्षीमालिङ्गच मोक्षं सुधियो लभन्ते ॥ आस्वादयन्तः पिशितस्य खण्डमाकण्ठपूर्ण च मुर्गा Pom: I amHE HAT Coat pri grad u AH verses 4 and 7. of 

EFT, D. C. ms. No. 959 of 1884–87 copled in Hora 1790, i, e, in 1734 A. D. This may be compared witb the doctrine of the Huerada quoted above ( p. 1073). There is a ms. in the BORI at Poona (D.C. No. 994 of 1891-1895) called TTARATUTafafa, which describes the sanctifications of five makāras with Vedic mantras on the same lines as the te farro cited above,|निधाय धारां वदने सुधायाः श्रीचक्रमभ्यर्च्य कुलक्रमेण। आस्वाद्य मद्यं पिशितं मृगाक्षीमालिङ्ग्य मोक्षं सुधियो लभन्ते ॥ आस्वादयन्तः पिशितस्य खण्डमाकण्ठपूर्ण च सुधां पिबन्तः । सुकेशणासङ्गतमाचरन्तो मुक्तिं च भुक्तिं च वयं व्रजामः ॥ verses 4 and 7 of कौलरहस्य, D. C. ms. No. 959 of 1884–87 copied in संवत् 1790, i. e. in 1734 A. D. This may be compared with the doctrine of the नीलपटदर्शन quoted above (p. 1073). There is a ms. in the BORI at Poona (D. C. No. 994 of 1891–1895) called पञ्चमकारशोधनविधि, which describes the sanctifications of five makāras with Vedic mantras on the same lines as the महानिर्वाणतन्त्र cited above.]]

[[P1088]]taken over in the initiations of bhoga by the devout and sensual helpmate. The initiating [[women play|woman plays]] the part of [[Sakti|Śakti]] while the male initiate assumes that of [[Siva|Śiva]] and both attain together to the realisation of the immanence within themselves of the consubstantiality of the Goddess and the God.' It has already been stated (p. 1054) that Prof. Zimmer is wrong in thinking that the Tāntrik rites were systematically disparaged as 'vāmamārga' by the Indian partisans of Yoga. He is also wrong (p. 130) when he says that 'throughout the first [[millenium|millennium]] A. D. they (Tāntric rites) were a basic element of normal Indian [[experi ence.'|experience.']] There is really very little evidence to substantiate this claim. He makes this sweeping generalisation as an art-critic and historian of Indian Art from the few tāntric sculptures on the Purī and other temples in Orissa and some other places in India. Vide the well-known work 'Bengal Lancer' by F. Yeats-Brown (London, Golancz, 1930) pp. [[23:-237,|235–237,]] where the superintendent of the temple of Jagannātha justifies the obscene frescoes on the ground that until a man is master of his gross body he cannot see the Godhead and feels sure that future ages will look on Tāntrik psychology with understanding.

One or two very peculiar notions and practices of the Tāntrikas may be briefly mentioned here. The offering of flesh, wine and mudrā to the devatā with the recital of three [[bijas|bījas]] 'āṁ, hrīm, krom' and the mantras 'om Ānandabhairavāya [[Damah'|namaḥ']] and 'om Ānandabhairavyai [[namah'|namaḥ']] was technically called [[suddhi|śuddhi]][^1762]. The Mahānirvāṇa and Tantrarāja-tantra state that to drink wine without [[suddhi 1763|śuddhi]][^1763]

In spite of these requirements of high qualities the रुद्रयामल (in 28. 31–36) provides that a वीर should honour the beautiful wife of another (or 'his own wife or another's') wearing charming ornaments whose body is suffused with erotic passion and who has become gay with wine 'अथ वीरो यजेत्कान्तां परकीयामथापि वा ।...मदनानलतप्ताङ्गीमासवासवमन्दविह्वलाम् ॥' &c. The महानिर्वाण० I. 57 refers to the three categories of साधक viz. पशु, वीर and दिव्य, the last being defined as दिव्यश्च देवताप्रायः शुद्धान्तःकरणः सदा । द्वन्द्वातीतो वीतरागः सर्वभूतसमः क्षमी ॥ (ibid. I. 55). The Tantras contain conflicting views on these three bhāvas. The Kālivilāsa-tantra says that men of divya type existed only in Satya-yuga and Tretāyuga, Vīra only in Tretā and Dvāpara and these two were non-existent in Kali and Paśu-bhāva remained in Kali (VI. 10 and 21).]]

[[P1089]]was like swallowing poison, that the person doing so would suffer from diseases for a long time, would soon die prematurely and that wine was to be drunk even by one who has attained some perfection only up till the mind has reached an ecstatic state of absorption (in thoughts of [[Dovi)|Devī)]] and that if he drinks beyond that stage he at once becomes a [[sinnor|sinner]] (vide also Kulārṇava [[VIL.|VII.]] 97–98 for the last proposition).

A most [[rovolting|revolting]] rite from the view-point of all [[unsophisti cated|unsophisticated]] people is what is called [['oakra-pājā'|'cakra-pūjā']] (worship in a circle). An equal number of men and women without distinction of caste and even near blood relations secretly meet at night and sit in a [[oircle|circle]] (vide [[Kaulāvallnirṇaya|Kaulāvalīnirṇaya]] VIII. 76). The Goddess is represented by a yantra (diagram). There is a leader of the cakra. The regulations were that only persons who had attained the status of Vīra (defined above in n 1763) were to be admitted[^1764] and paśus (ordinary men with beastly passions not curbed) were to be excluded. What assurance was there that the leader of the cakra himself possessed the noble qualities mentioned in the [[vorse|verse]] quoted above and would choose only men possessed of similar qualities ? The women assembled cast their [[bodicos|bodices]] in [[&|a]] receptacle and each of the assembled men [[gocurod|secured]] a female companion for that night by lot [[i. o,|i. e.]] by taking a bodice out of those contained in the receptacle. The practice of

[^1764]: [[Vide Sakti and sākta' p. 354, Farquhar's Outlines of the religions Literature of India' p. 203, hetlatafory VIII, 204-219, men पसान्त शुभ वा यदि वायुभम् । कदाचिव वक्तव्यमित्याज्ञा परमेम्परि। कुलधर्माविक सर्व सर्वावस्थासु सर्वदा। गोपयेच प्रयस्मेन जननीजारगर्भवत् । वेदशासपुराणानि स्पटानि.गणिका nir a prut TOT THT FOREN FIRE XI. 79, 84, 85. In the pat forefror (IV. 79-80), however, siva is made to say that the Kaalika-sadhana should be made openly and that what he said in other tantras about the concealment of Kaplikadbarma aot being blamable was not applicable when tho Kali age becamo powerful.|Vide 'Śakti and Śākta' p. 354, Farquhar's 'Outlines of the religious Literature of India' p. 203, महानिर्वाणतन्त्र VIII. 204–219. चक्रवृत्तान्तं शुभं वा यदि वाशुभम् । कदाचिन्नेव वक्तव्यमित्याज्ञा परमेश्वरि ॥ कुलधर्मादिकं सर्वं सर्वावस्थासु सर्वदा । गोपयेच्च प्रयत्नेन जननीजारगर्भवत् ॥ वेदशास्त्रपुराणानि स्पष्टानि गणिका इव । इयं तु शाम्भवी विद्या गुप्ता कुलवधूरिव ॥ कुलार्णव XI. 79, 84, 85. In the महानिर्वाण (IV. 79–80), however, Śiva is made to say that the Kaulika-sādhana should be made openly and that what he said in other tantras about the concealment of Kaulikadharma not being blamable was not applicable when the Kali age became powerful.]]

[[P1090]]Śrīcakra must have given rise to great obloquy and unpopularity for the Tāntrikas. Therefore, the Kulārṇava[^1765] advises that cakrapūjā should be *sub rosa*. 'What happens at Śrīcakra, whether good or bad, should never be uttered (in public); this is the order (of God); one should never give out information about what happens at Cakrapūjā'. The 18th century Mahānirvāṇatantra, which is reformist in character, states that, since in the Kali age (in which people are weak and the influence of sinful age is very strong), for the last tattva (i. e. maithuna) one's own wife is alone to be the Śakti because in that case no fault can be found or some substitute like red sandalwood paste may be employed. In the author's youth whispers floated about that in some towns in Mahārāṣṭra cakrapūjā was practised, that even great Pandits thought that their learning was due to the favour of Devī, that they, though very orthodox in other respects, took once a year in Devīpūjā a thimbleful of wine as *prasāda*. The learned Achyutarāya[^1766] Modak of Nasik composed at Nasik a work called 'Avaidikadhikkṛti' (condemnation of non-vaidika practices) wherein he severely handled the cult of the five makāras.

Naturally common people, who could not appreciate or understand the abstruse and subtle philosophy of Śakti, Nāda, Bindu and so forth, seized with avidity upon the apparently easy path of worship of Śakti by the five makāras and the mantras, bījas, cakras and the like taught by the Tantras and it has been seen above (pp. 1073–76) to what depths some persons professing to be gurus, Śāktas and Tāntrikas descended in course of time.

The path of the Tantras was in its higher level one of *Upāsanā* or *Bhakti*, though it very often degenerated into magic and moral depravity. The deity worshipped viz. Parameśvarī

[^1765]: शेषतत्त्वं महेशानि निर्वीर्ये प्रबले कलौ । स्वकीया केवलं ज्ञेया सर्वदोषविवर्जिता ॥ अथवा स्वयम्भावि कुसुमं प्राणवल्लभे । कथितं तत्प्रतिनिधौ कुसीदं परिकीर्तितम् ॥ महानिर्वाण० VI. 14–15; अत्र means 'in offering शेषतत्त्व' (i. e. the 5th). The com. explains 'कुसीदं रक्तचन्दनम्'. Women that could be śaktis were of three classes, स्वीया (one's own wife), परकीया (wife of another) and साधारणी (a woman who is a veśyā).

[^1766]: Vide for Achyutarāya Modak 'Taraporevala Commemoration volume' of the Deccan College Research Institute, pp. 214–220. The work was finished at Pañcavaṭī in śaka 1736 Phālguna bright half 10th (i. e. in 1815 A. D.).

[[P1091]]presented three aspects to the upāsaka[^1767] [[( devotee)|(devotee)]] viz. gross (sthūla), subtle (sūkṣma) and para (highest). The first aspect is represented by the form of [[Devi|Devī]] with hands, feet and other limbs, which is fit for worship with the hands and the eyes of the devotee; the second aspect consists of mantras, which are fit for apprehension with the organs of hearing and speech by those who have the good fortune to receive the mantras from [[&|a]] worthy guru. The third aspect (para) is one to be apprehended by the mind of the sādhaka and described as [[all-poryading|all-pervading]] consciousness and the like (in [[Nityaṣodasika|Nityaṣoḍaśikā]] VI. 49-50).

Some modern writers have [[beon|been]] somewhat unjust to the whole [[Tantrik|Tāntrik]] literature in labelling it all as black magic or as full of obscenities. The present writer is not one of those who [[some times|sometimes]] hold that what is not understood is either false, absurd or non-existent. He is prepared to believe that the end and aim of a few of the higher minds among Tāntrikas and of some of the works on Tantra was the attainment of high spiritual powers by Yoga practices, the Realization of the Supreme Tattva (Principle) variously designated as Brahma, Viṣṇu, [[śiva|Śiva]] or [[Dovi|Devī]], and Liberation (mokṣa). He is aware that many of these claim to be based on Vedic traditions, teachings and practices and to have further developed the conceptions underlying Vedic teachings and ritual and that even the [[magio|magic]] rites contained in [[Boveral|several]] Tāntrik works had their counterparts, though on a much smaller scale, in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]], the [[Atharyaveda|Atharvaveda]], [[Samavidbina|Sāmavidhāna]] Brāhmaṇa and other Vedic works. The present author, though he has carefully studied many of the Tantras and the [[Yogasutra|Yogasūtra]] of [[Patanjali|Patañjali]] with the bhāṣya and commentaries thereon, has to admit that he has had no mystic experience, but he is not [[pre pared|prepared]] to deny that prophets, saints, poets and others might have [[mystio|mystic]] visions and experiences. Man's psychic powers are vast

[^1767]: [[suratigrerat: Tragetaltfor surguureitratto put out of a|अथात्रोपास्यायाः परमेश्वर्यास्त्रीणि रूपाण्युपास्तियोग्यानि स्थूलं सूक्ष्मं परं चेति ।]] [[तत्रार्थ|तत्राद्यं]] करचरणाद्यवयवशीलं मन्त्रसिद्धिमतां चक्षुरिन्द्रियपाणीन्द्रिययोर्योग्यम् । ततः [[पथनिर्मा|पद्मनिभां]] देवीं [[पालार्ककिरणारुणाम्,|बालार्ककिरणारुणाम्,]] इत्यादिना [[पक्ष्यमाणम्|वक्ष्यमाणम्]] । द्वितीयं [[मन्त्रात्मक|मन्त्रात्मकं]] [[पुण्यवता|पुण्यवतां]] [[श्रवणेखिय पाणिन्द्रिययोर्योग्यम्|श्रवणेन्द्रिय-वागिन्द्रिययोर्योग्यम्]] । ... तृतीयं [[पासनात्मक|वासनात्मकं]] पुण्यवतां मनसो [[योग्य-चैतन्यमात्मनो|योग्यम् — चैतन्यमात्मनो]] रूपम् इत्यादिना [[षठे|षष्ठे]] पटले वक्ष्यमाणम् । [[एतत्रितयातीतं तुपाबनसाती सुत्केरईतयाऽनुभूयमानमखण्ड|एतत्त्रितयातीतं तु वाङ्मनसातीतं मुक्तेरर्हतयाऽनुभूयमानमखण्डं]] रूपम् । सेतुबन्ध on [[निस्याषोडशिका.|नित्याषोडशिका०]] pp. 7-8. The words [[चैवग्यमात्मनो|चैतन्यमात्मनो]] रूपम् occur [[ja fareutanate to|in नित्याषोडशिका०]] VI. 50 p. 213. [[Porn is ' cari TTTHT piha urefu Quais happu on p. 25.|Nitya is 'अपारमेशिवाभिन्ना शक्तिरेव यावन्नित्ये-त्युच्यते।' सेतुबन्ध on p. 25.]] The [[fare tutte lerato|नित्याषोडशिका०]] (I. 138-160) contains a finely worded description of the gross aspect of [[Devi|Devī]] [[beglaning|beginning]] with the [[bali varsa 'ad: Tufant et arrestato T.|half verse 'ततः पद्मनिभां देवीं बालार्ककिरणारुणाम्.']]

[[P1092]]and unknown as propounded in Alexis [[Carrell's|Carrel's]] work 'Man the unknown' and in 'Invisible influence' by Dr. Alexander Cannon (15th impression, Rider and Co., London, 1935). He knows that some of the Tāntrik works made a distinction between the rules of ordinary social life and conventions (samājā-dharma) and the peculiar forms of Tāntrik worship, in which, while it lasts, no [[diatinction|distinction]] is made on the ground of caste or [[Box|sex]] (vide notes 1711 and 1713). It may further be conceded that the Tantra works placed women on a footing of equality with men, gave them an exalted position and that they endeavoured to provide a common platform (as shown in n. 1704 above) for differing and wrangling sects of Vaiṣṇavas, Śaivas and others by putting forward [[Dovi|Devī]] as the object of worship for all; but they had not much success as is shown by the [[fact|facts]] that Vaiṣṇavas and Śaivas still carried on their quarrels and that the Tāntrik texts themselves fell into five classes, viz. Śaiva, Śākta, [[Vaisnava|Vaiṣṇava]], Saura and Gāṇapatya and that there are differing doctrines among the Tāntrikas called Kādimata, Hādimata [[&o.|&c.]]

The matters that distinguish Tāntrik works from other religious literature in Sanskrit are the [^1768] promise of the attainment of miraculous powers, the Realization of the one Supreme Principle in [[&|a]] short time by means of the Tāntrik sādhanā [[ " method|'method]] or [[procedure '|procedure']] (vide Intro. to 'Principles of Tantra' p. XIV), by their insistence on the worship of [[Devi|Devī]] with makāras alone as yielding the desired results (as in [[Maha. nirvāṇa|Mahānirvāṇa]] V. 24 '[[paīcatattva-vibināyām|pañcatattva-vihīnāyām]] pūjāyām na phalod-

[^1768]: Sir [[Jobo|John]] Woodroffe remarks (in the [[latrodaction|Introduction]] to his [[. Priaci ples|'Principles]] of Tantra' part 2, pp. XII-XIV) that the one topic that appears to [[diferentiate|differentiate]] Tantras from other religious works is the [[constitaent|constituent]] parts of its ritual such as the mantras, [[bijas, modrās|bījas, mudrās]], yantras, [[bhutasuddhi|bhūtaśuddhi]] and that it is mainly by these items rather than by anything else that [[tho tāatrik|the tāntrik]] character of a work is established. Vide also [[the Sāktas.|'the Śāktas']] by E. [[A,|A.]] Payne p. 137 for a similar view. Sir[[, Jobo| John]] Woodroffe, while reviewing [[Payao's|Payne's]] work (in JRAS for 1935 at p. 387), himself agreed that what distinguished the Śākta ritual is the [[maptra|mantra]] and magical sections and that part of it which deals with the secret ritual, that, while there is ordinarily [[do thoga|no bhoga]] (enjoyment) where there is yoga, in the Śākta doctrine a man may [[hava borb|have both]] yoga and bhoga and that this is a [[distiact|distinct]] and profound characteristic of that doctrine. Even the Buddhist [[Vajrayapa Tantra porport|Vajrayāna Tantras purport]] to aim at the [[attaiament|attainment]] of bodhi (vide [[Gahyasamāja|Guhyasamāja]] p. 154, [[Sadhanamālā|Sādhanamālā]] I. p. 225 and II. [[P.|p.]] 421) and [[ARE 14 # T: HATGIT: H r vats i a पशान्ति पर बोधिजन्मनी हैव साधका|ज्ञानसिद्धि I. 4 'ये तु सत्त्वाः समाख्याताः सर्वसङ्कल्पवर्जिताः । ते स्पृशन्ति परां बोधिं जन्मनीहैव साधकाः ॥']]

[[P1093]][[bhavah')|bhavaḥ')]] and on their peculiar ritual of mantras, [[bijas|bījas]] (syllables meaningless to ordinary men), [[nyāgas,|nyāsas,]] mudrās, cakras, yantras and similar things for attaining their goal. The condemnation poured on Tāntricism is principally due to their insistence on wine, meat and sexual unions as the best and the only means for the effective worship of [[Devi,|Devī,]] their theory that by merely repeating some mantra or mantras over wine, meat and other tattvas, by offering them to [[Devi|Devī]] and by meditation on her, one may drink wine or eat meat, [[whon|when]] in the same breath they say emphatically that partaking of wine and meat is sinful without this ritual. This antagonizes those who are not kaulas and who hold that this persistent teaching is very dangerous for the common run of [[mon|men]] and savours of hypocrisy.

Some of the Tantras practically inculcated what appears to non-tāntrikas unbridled licence. The [[Kaulāvali-nirṇaya|Kaulāvalī-nirṇaya]] (IV. 15 ff) asserts: 'The [[Sāktas|Śāktas]] have no higher means of happiness and liberation than the fifth tattva (i. e. maithuna); [[&|a]] sādhaka becomes siddha only by the [[practice )|(practice)]] of the fifth [[tattve.|tattva.]] If he resorts only to the first (i. e. wine) he becomes only Bhairava, if only to the 2nd (meat) he becomes [[Brahmi,|Brahmā,]] by the third (fish) he becomes [[& mahabhairava,|a mahābhairava,]] by the 4th ([[mudra|mudrā]]) he becomes foremost among [[sādhakan.179|sādhakas.]] [^1769] The same Tantra [[goos|goes]] further and frankly says 'all women are fit for intercourse to a (Śākta) worshipper except the wives of his guru or of those [[sāktas|Śāktas]] who have attained to the status of [[Vira,|Vīra,]] that for those who have reached the stage of [[advaja|advaya]] there is no prohibition [[por|nor]] is anything enjoined. To the pure everything is pure, it is only the hankering that is [[blamable.|blamable.']] In this connection that work advances certain puerile and obscene arguments (in VIII. 223-225) about illicit or incestuous intercourse that cannot be set out here. That tantra does not stand alone in [[suob|such]] statements. For example, the [[Kālivilāsatantra|Kālīvilāsatantra]] (X. 20-21) allows adulterous intercourse to a '[[Sākta|Śākta']] devotee pro-

[[faute|कौलावलीनिर्णय]] IV. 15-16; [[Tratay Fit Toy: Hatua tigrast gar auf rata सन्दिग्धानां च सर्वदा।|गुरुवीरवधूस्त्यक्त्वा रम्याः सर्वाश्च योषितः । एता वर्ज्याः प्रयत्नेन सन्दिग्धानां च सर्वदा ॥]] [[अदैतानां|अद्वैतानां]] च कुत्रापि निषेधो नैव विद्यते । ... [[अत एव थवा पस्य बासमा कुत्सिता भवेत् । सवा दोषाय भवति नान्यथा पूषण कचित्।|अत एव यदा यस्य वासना कुत्सिता भवेत् । तदा दोषाय भवति नान्यथा दूषणं क्वचित् ॥]] कौलावलीनिर्णय VIII. 221–222, 226; [[The proposta a Ha Hereti|पवित्रं सकलं चैव वासना कुत्सिता भवेत् ।]] ibid. XVII. 170. [[Fuga: परनारी|दीक्षितः परनारीषु]] यदि मैथुनमाचरेत् । [[न विन्दोः|न बिन्दोः]] पातनं कार्य [[कुले चमहा|कृते च ब्रह्महा]] भवेत् । [[पदिन प्रपतेष चिया परमारी|यदि न स्रवते बिन्दुः परनारीषु]] पार्वति । [[सर्वसिद्धीपरो|सर्वसिद्धीश्वरो]] भूत्वा विहरेद् [[भूमिमण्डले|भूममण्डले ॥]] कालीविलासतन्त्र [[20-21.|X. 20-21.]]

[[P1094]]vided it is not carried to the last stage of emission and avers that, if he observes the condition in the proviso, he would become the master of supernatural powers in spite of adultery. It should be noted that the author of this work has unabashed hardihood to make Śiva tell this to [[Pārvati.|Pārvatī.]] About wine that work remarks 'just as drinking of soma[^1770] is prescribed for brāhmaṇas [[is|in]] solemn Vedic sacrifices, similarly wine should be drunk at proper times (or according to the practice of Kaulas), since it confers enjoyment as well as mokṣa; drinking wine is blamable in the case of those who hanker after benefits or who are egoistic; but in the case of those that are free from egoism there is neither prohibition (of drinking) nor the enjoining of it. One who is free from the fetters of making distinctions should practise drinking wine for the purpose of remembering the meaning of the mantras and for making his mind fixed (on worship) but he who resorts to wine, and other tattvas merely for pleasure is sinful.' The teachings of works like the [[Kaulāvali-nirnaya|Kaulāvalī-nirṇaya]] about drinking wine and sexual intercourse with all sorts of women as the highest means of [[Sakti|Śakti]] worship by persons professing to be advaitins led to great depravity and sexual immorality and orgies as indicated by the criticism in medieval works cited above (pp. 1073–76) that regarded Tāntrik teachings as execrable, though some medieval Hindu works admitted into religious practices nyāsa, mudrā, yantra and the like, which were deemed to be innocuous and which would be described in this work later on. The mere intention of doing good if certain conditions were fulfilled is not enough excuse, when it is most likely that the means proposed for attaining a high spiritual level and for liberation would have the opposite effect on most people. Taking all things into consideration, the present author is constrained to observe that medieval and recent writers who severely criticized Tāntrik works were on the whole largely justified in their condemnation of Tāntrik practices as enunciated in many Tāntrik works and of the works themselves. For one man that attained super-normal powers, high spiritual level and great mystic experience there must have been hundreds of hypocrites, charlatans, and licentious men who deluded trustful and ignorant men and particularly women.

[^1770]: [[यथा ऋतुषु विप्राणां|यथा क्रतुषु विप्राणां]] सोमपानं विधीयते । मद्यपानं तथा कार्य समये भोगमोक्षदम् ॥ फलार्थिनामहङ्कारवतां दूषणमेव हि । निरहङ्कारिणां चैव न निषेधो न वा विधिः ॥ [[कौलावली|कौलावली०]] VIII. 90-91; मन्त्रार्थस्मरणार्थं तु मनसः स्थैर्यहेतवे । [[भेवपाशनिर्मुक्तो|भेदपाशनिर्मुक्तो]] मद्यपानं समाचरेत् । सेवेत यः सुखार्थाय [[मध्यादीनि|मद्यादीनि]] स पातकी ॥ ibid. VIII. 74.

[[P1095]]Only a few Purāṇas like the [[Devipurāṇa,|Devīpurāṇa,]] the [[· Kalika,|Kālikā,]] the [[Devimahatmya|Devīmāhātmya]] in the [[Markandega|Mārkaṇḍeya]] provide for the employment of some of the condemned makāras (madya, [[mārga,|māṁsa,]] matsya) in the worship of the Great Goddess. From about the 6th or 7th century A.D. [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] began to incorporate the special ceremonial characteristics of the [[Saktas|Śāktas]] and Tāntrikas. Aparārka quotes a passage [^1771] from the [[Devipurāṇa|Devīpurāṇa]] wherein the qualifications of a Sthāpaka (one who [[performis Dovapratiṣthā)|performs Devapratiṣṭhā)]] are set out, viz. he would be the best [[sthāpaka|Sthāpaka]] for establishing images of [[Devi|Devī]] and the [[Matrs,|Mātṛs,]] who knows the vāma (left or opposite) and the dakṣiṇa (right) path of worship, who has thoroughly mastered the veda relating to the [[Mātrs|Mātṛs]] (the Mother Goddesses), who is [[olever|clever]] in the interpretation of [[Panoarātra|Pañcarātra]] works and is proficient in the Tantras of the [[Matrs|Mātṛs]] &c. The [[Kālika-purāṇa|Kālikā-purāṇa]] devotes many chapters (54 ff) to the [[desoription|description]] of mantras, kavacas, mudrās, nyāsas &c. The [[Bhagavatapurāṇa|Bhāgavatapurāṇa]] also and Agni[^1772] 372.34 expressly say that the worship of gods and of [[Vignu|Viṣṇu]] also is either [[vaidiki,|vaidikī,]] [[tāntriki|tāntrikī]] or [[mitrā,|miśrā,]] the first and third being for the three higher varṇas and the [[tantriki|tāntrikī]] for [[sūdras.|śūdras.]] The [[Bhagavatapurāṇa|Bhāgavatapurāṇa]] refers to the worship of Keśava laid down in the Tantras for him who desires to cast out the knot (bondage or grief) of the heart. It ([[Bhagavata)|Bhāgavata)]] also mentions[^1773] [[Vaidiki|Vaidikī]] and [[Tantriki|Tāntrikī]] [[dikṣā|dīkṣā]] (in XI. 11. 37) and refers to the Tāntrik method of the [[angas,|aṅgas,]] [[upāngas,|upāṅgas,]] [[Āyudhas|āyudhas]] and decorations in the worship of the Lord of [[Laksml. 1774|Lakṣmī.]] [^1774] Some of the [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] and medieval nibandhas, however, fully utilized what Tantras [[bad|had]] to say about mantras, japa, nyāsa, [[mandala,|maṇḍala,]] cakra, yantra and

[^1771]: [[171. थवपि देवीपुराणे-घामदक्षिणवेचा|यद्यपि देवीपुराणे — वामदक्षिणवेत्ता]] यो मातृवेदार्थपारगः । स भवेत्स्थापकः [[et fantai Alyt( Tl) & TIT T A HIGGST inq: 1|श्रेष्ठो देवीनां मात्रृणां ( मातृकाणां ? ) तु च । पञ्चरात्रार्थकुशलो मातृमन्त्रविशारदः ।]] &c. [[Sprin|अपरार्क]] p. 16, who then quotes Matsya 265. 1-5 for the qualifications of sthāpaka in which there is no reference to [[बाम,|वाम,]] दक्षिण or तन्त्र. This and the quotations from the [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] indicate that the Matsya was composed some [[centurios:|centuries]] before the [[ganger and raayam.|देवीपुराण and भागवतपुराण.]]

[^1772]: [[पौदिकस्तान्त्रिको मिश्री विष्णो त्रिविधी मखः । त्रयाणामीप्सितेनैकविधिना हरि Hernal sy 372.34|वैदिकस्तान्त्रिको मिश्र इति त्रिविधो मखः । त्रयाणामीप्सितेनैव विधिना हरिमर्चयेत् । अग्नि 372. 34.]]

[^1773]: [[य आहवयगन्धि निजिहार्पः|य आशु हृदयग्रन्थिं निर्जिहीर्षुः]] परात्मनः । [[विधिनोपचरेद|विधिनोपचरेद्]] देवं तन्त्रोक्तेन च [[केशवम|केशवम्]] ॥ [[लब्धाइग्रह आचार्याचेन|लब्धानुग्रह आचार्यात्तेन]] [[सन्दर्शितागमः।|सन्दशितागमः ।]] [[पिण्ड विशोध्य|पिण्डं विशोध्य]] [[संन्यासकृतरक्षोर्चयधरिम्|संन्यासकृतरक्षोऽर्चयेद्धरिम्]] ॥ [[na|भागवत]] XI. 3. 47 and 49. Here [[forepitva|पिण्डशोधन]] refers to the [[gre|भूतशुद्धि]] prescribed [[ja|in]] such [[afegn|तान्त्रिक]] works as [[her paretoran|महानिर्वाणतन्त्र]] (V. 93–105) and taken over in late medieval works like the [[T T|पूजाप्रकाश]] pp. 129–133 and [[TF|न्यास]] also is mentioned [[AD A Moans|as a means]] of protection against evil.

[[P1096]]similar matters. This will be illustrated later by some examples. Even for such a simple and common topic as the 16 [[upaoiras|upacāras]] of worship, the [[Varsakriya-kaumudi|Varṣakriyā-kaumudī]] (p. 156) and [[Ekādasi-tattva|Ekādaśī-tattva]] (p. 59) quote the [[Prapañasāra-tantra|Prapañcasāra-tantra]] (VI. 41-42).

The Purāṇas and some [[smptis|smṛtis]] prescribe short mantras of five, six, eight, twelve, thirteen and more syllables as very efficacious for securing all objects. A few of them are set out in the note [^1775] below. Medhātithi [[or|on]] Manu says that the word mantra primarily means a part of the Veda comprising [[Rgveda,|Ṛgveda,]] Yajurveda and [[Sānaveda,|Sāmaveda,]] treated as such by those who have studied the Veda, and that expressions like "Agnaye [[svāhā "|svāhā"]] [^1776] employed in rites like Vaiśvadeva are called [[mantra.|mantras]] in a secondary sense by way of praise. The vedic conception was that a mantra [[bas|has]] great potency and that it must be correctly repeated to secure the desired result, that a mantra defective [[ad|as]] to [[accept|accent]] or as to a letter or wrongly applied did not convey the meaning intended and that becoming a thunderbolt in the form of a word or words it destroys [^1777] the [[yajamūna.|yajamāna.]] Vedic mantras are

[^1775]: Vide [[SIRETTES|शारदातिलक]] I. 73 [[8|ff]] for references to mantras of five or [[mora|more]] letters. A [[pañcakmara|pañcākṣara]] mantra is [[77: Frere|नमः शिवाय]] (in [[posao|लिङ्गपुराण]] I. 85); the same becomes a [[waotra|mantra]] of six letters when 'om' is [[prebxed.|prefixed.]] Other mantras of six letters are : [[wat facota|ओं नमो विष्णवे]] (in [[Toner|वृद्धहारीतस्मृति]] VI. 213), [[Faiter|ओं नमो हराय]] (in [[io la on a|हेमाद्रि on व्रत]] vol. I. p. 227), [[BITTATTACIAA|श्रीरामरामेरामेति]] (vide [[cote|note]] 219 above) and two more on p. 434 [[poder|under]] 'Ṣaḍakṣaramantra'; [['Kbakbolkāya samah'|'Khakholkāya namaḥ']] is a [[sadakara|ṣaḍakṣara]] mantra of Āditya quoted from [[wategtrus|भविष्यपुराण]] in [[TATT.|हेमाद्रि]] ([[II.|व्रत II.]] p. 521) and in [[Kalpatara|Kalpataru]] on vrata p. 9 and 199 (in the latter it is called [[a poder|मूलमन्त्र under]] Nimbasaptamī, the description of [[wbich|which]] is taken from [[Bbavigya, Brahma|Bhaviṣya, Brāhma-parva,]] chap. 215 and 216); mantras of eight letters are: [[Tatry|ओं नमो नारायणाय]] (in [[cytro|नारदपुराण]] I. 16. 38–39, [[Wagrio|ब्रह्मपुराण]] 60. 24, [[19.|वराहपु.]] 120. 7), [[af at bare|ओं नमो वासुदेवाय]] (in [[THTHFHTEE|वैखानसस्मार्तसूत्र]] IV. 12, [[THES.|नरसिंहपु.]] 63 6, q. by [[Bructi,|अपरार्क,]] [[HETTE|मत्स्यपु.]] 102. 4 q. by [[rgina.|स्मृतिच.]] I. p. 182 as [[onen);|मूलमन्त्र);]] a mantra of twelve letters is [[dat wat we eruare|ओं नमो भगवते वासुदेवाय]] (in [[arte.|नारदपु.]] I. 16. 38–39, [[athy.|नरसिंहपु.]] 7. 43); for a mantra of 13 letters about [[tra,|राम,]] vide note 219 above); mantras of 16 letters are [[stiltsporene Tot qe|गोपीजनवल्लभचरणं शरणं प्रपद्ये]] (in [[mag.|नारदपु.]] II. 59. 44) and [[ū HR Tafa starp E RIIET|ह्रीं गौरि रुद्रदयिते योगेश्वरि हूं फट् स्वाहा]] (in [[ancet.|शारदा०]] IX. 99).

[^1776]: [[Helena Sailarena pemisah RTEETAIathiya|मन्त्रशब्देन देवतोद्देशशब्दवान् स्वाहाकारान्तोऽग्नये स्वाहेत्येवमादिर्भण्योच्यते ।]] [[मपन्ये मात्रा|न हाम्ने मन्त्रा]] वैश्वदेवेषु विनियुक्तास्तेषु च मन्त्रत्वं [[प्रशंसयोग्यते।|प्रशंसांतयोच्यते ।]] [[न|न तु]] स्वाध्यायेऽपठितानां [[मन्त्रलमस्ति।|मन्त्रत्वमस्ति ।]] स्वाध्यायैकदेशः [[कश्विग्यतु:सामारमको पेयाच्यायिभिर्मन्त्र|कश्चिदृग्यजुःसामात्मको वेदाध्यायिभिर्मन्त्र]] इति [[ग्यवाहियते।|व्यवह्रियते ।]] [[594€Tau 997084 WE I AUTATU OD AE 111.|व्यवहारतश्च पदार्थावगमनम् । मेधातिथि on मनु III.]] 121 ([[io|in]] Dr. [[Jba's|Jha's]] edition).

[[11.|II.]] p. 347 and note 840, [[whero varāo|where verse]] 52 from the [[Paginiyasiksa|Pāṇinīyaśikṣā]] is [[quoled|quoted]] and the story of the result of wrong accentuation of the word [['jadrasatrab' ta briety|'indrasatruḥ' is briefly]] set out from Tai. S. II. 4. 12. 1 and [[Sat,|Śat.]] Br. I. 6. 3. 8-16.

[[P1097]]of four classes viz. [[Țk|ṛk]] (which is metrical), yajus (which has no restriction as to metre but which must be a sentence), [[&|a]] [[saman|sāman]] (which is sung) and nigada (i. [[o.|e.]] [[praiga,|praiṣa,]] meaning words that are addressed by one person to another calling upon the latter to do a certain act, e. g. the words 'srucaḥ [[sammrddhi,|sammṛddhi,]] [[proksanir āsādaya'.|prokṣaṇīr-āsādaya'.]] Nigadas are yajus in form but are distinguished from yajus by the fact that the former are loudly uttered, while a yajus is ordinarily recited in a low voice. [^1778] The most sacred mantra is the [[Gāyatri|Gāyatrī]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] III. 62. 10 'tat-savitur' &c.). The [[Atharvavada|Atharvaveda]] (XIX. 71. 1) calls it Vedamātā (the mother of the Veda). The [[Bľahadāraṇyakopaniṣad|Bṛhadāraṇyakopaniṣad]] (V. 14) contains a grand eulogy of the [[Gāyatri.|Gāyatrī.]] [^1779] Om is a very sacred [[syllablo, &|syllable, a]] symbol of brahma and may be called in the language of the Tantras a [[bija.|bīja.]] There are only a few syllables such as om, [[phat,|phaṭ,]] [[vaṣat|vaṣaṭ]] in the Vedic literature that on the face of them have no meaning but are like [[bija|bīja]] mantras in the Tantra sense. There is a [[Bijanighaṇķu|Bījanighaṇṭu]] (a dictionary of [[bija|bīja]] mantras) printed in [[' Tantrik|'Tāntrik]] texts' Vol. I. pp. 28–29 (where monosyllabic [[bijas|bījas]] such as [[Hrim, Srim, Krim,|Hrīm, Śrīm, Krīm,]] Hum, [[phat,|phaṭ,]] are set out and described in symbolic words indicated in note 1708 above. It is stated about a dozen times in the Aitareya Brāhmaṇa viz. it is the perfection of sacrifice when it is [[rūpasamddha (|rūpasamṛddha (]]perfect as to its [[form )|form)]] [[i, e.|i. e.]] when the [[fk|ṛk]] verse pointedly refers to the sacrificial act that is being performed. [^1780] The [[Nirukta.(I.|Nirukta (I.]] 15–16) starts a lengthy discussion on Kautsa's view that mantras have no sense (or are purposeless). There is a long discussion in the [[Pūrvamlmāmgāsūtra|Pūrvamīmāṁsāsūtra]] (I. 2. 31 ff) on the [[name|same]] lines [[88|as]] in the Nirukta. Jaimini states that there is no difference in the meanings of words employed in the Veda and those employed by people and [[Sabara|Śabara]] adds in his bhāṣya (on P. M. S. I. 2. 32) that mantras are recited in sacrifices only for the purpose of conveying or manifesting the meaning. [^1781] It is difficult to

Br. 'yat karma kriyamāṇam [[rgabhivadati'|ṛgabhivadati']] contain the same doctrine that the PMS and [[Sabara|Śabara]] emphasize [[viz,|viz.]] mantras are [['.artbapratyāyaka'|'arthapratyāyaka']] or 'arthaprakāśaka'.
